yet had. The Queen has a great affection for Irish poplin, and has again ordered several lengths of this fabric for wear during the spring. The Princess of I Wales too, 'has always shown a preference for Irish • poplin, several toilettes in her Indian outfit being built of this material. Lady Aberdeen, who makes a great point of donning fabrics and trimmings of Irish manu- facture, wore for the State entry into Dublin a black poplin gown trimmed with Irish sequin embroidery, a very handsome and effective combination. New Sleeves.—There is one point a woman who can afford but few blouses in the year should carefully con- sider, and that is—the sleeves. Provided these are moderately up-to-date the rest will pass. When making or having a blouse made, a yard or two of the material should always be kept back for sleeve renovations. This season will see fresh changes in sleeve-land, the huge puffs from shoulder to elbow being on the wane. The coming sleeve is the" nicked up" specimen, which ' is slightly puffed at the shoulder and quite tight from elbow to wrist. Forthcoming Novels.—Admirers of Miss Beatrice Harraden will rejoice to hear that a new novel from her pen, entitled "The Se,holar's Daughter" will shortly appear. Messrs. Methuen are the publishers, and by the way, this firm has just issued yet another edition of Miss Harraden's "In Varying Moods" Messrs. Methuen are this week publishing a new novel by Eden •Phillpotts entitled "The Portreen " and a new 6d. book "The Wild Duck Shooter " by Dumas. MADGE.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Court Meurning.—The death of Kidg Christian ils thrown a gloom over all social engagements for the ei rly part of the London season. That was inevitable .iii der the circumstances, but King Edward, in limiting the period of Court mourning to six weeks, and adhering to his engagement to open Parliament in person, is evidently desirous that as little alteration as possible should be made in tho existing arrangements for the coming season. The two Courts, which were to have been given at Buckingham Palacethis month, have been postponed, and the great bereavement which has fallen upon the Queen must necessarily alter her Majesty's plans for some time to come, but the death of the King of Denmark will not have such a disastrous effect upon the London season as it would have done had it come at a later date.

The New King of Denmark.—The Royal Family of Denmark is noted for its simplicity, and the new King Frederick VIII. is as unaffected in his ways and manners as was his aged aid distinguished father. King Christian came from a singularly long-lived family, and he was one of ten children whose average age was well over seventy, whilst his late Connort, who died in 1898, belonged to a family almost as long-lived. The new King was born in 1813, being nearly two years younger than our own King Edward. and is a well set-up, soldierly-looking man, who carries his years very lightly. He is very popular with the Danish people, both for his philanthropic disposition, and his earnest endeavours to promote the best interests of his country. He married, in 1889, Princess Louise of Sweden, and has eight children, one of whom, Prince Charles, is now King Haakon, of Norway. The Kaiser and the London Specialist. —The German Emperor, who will celebrate his silver wedding on the 17th February, and on the same day will attend the wedding of his second son Prince Eitel Fritz, is suffer lug from renewed trouble in the left ear, and has called in a Londoa specialist to see if anything can be done for his increasing deafness. This is an infirmity which has long troubled the Emperor but the summoning of a London aurist is not regarded with favour in Germany, any more than was the calling in Sir Morell Mackenzie to attend to the throat disease of the late Emperor Frederick. The present Emperor William was one of the first to proclaim, in that agitation, that, if German surgical science could not prevail, it was no use calling is the foreigner, and now with somewhat tragic irony, the Kaiser has himself had to resort to the advice of a London specialist. A Notable Silver Wedding.—The Baroness Bnrdett- Coutts will this year celebrate her silver wedding, a happy event she could hardly have looked forward to, when, at the age of sixty-seven she married Mr. Burdett-Coutts in 1881. That event was quite the sensation of the time, for though Miss Coutts, as she was until made a peeress in 1871, bad received hundreds of offers, no one thought in those days she would ever marry. Lord Crewe, the father of the present peer, tells in some memoir's how he thought Miss Coutts liked him because he had never proposed to her, but he went on to say that almost all the young men of good family did, and that a great many did so out of duty to their family. That was before the advent of American heiresses and millionaires, and moneyed women were few and seldom in those days. So many were the pro- posals to Miss Coutts. that, according to the late Lord Crewe, her companion knew exactly when they were coming, and used obligingly to leave the room for a few minutes, which was all that was necessary. Great was the surprise then when she married her secretary, who for a long time had charge of her many philanthropic schemes. Mr. Burdett-Coutts who sits in the House of Commons as Unionist member for West- minster, and was recently re-elected. American born, he and his brother, the late Sir Ellis Ashmead-Bartlett, having been brought to this country when boys, at the time of the American Civil War.

Another Crichton-Stuart Wedding.—Like his brother, the Marquis of Bute, Lord Ninian Crichton-Stuart has gene to Ireland for his bride, and is to marry the Hon. Ismay Preston, the only daughter of Lord and Lady Gormanston. It is another illustration of the old say- ine that one wedding makes another, as Miss Preston was one or,the young Marchioness of Butes bridesmaids last summer. The Bates are related to nearly all the Catholic nobility, and there has been a long standing friendship between them and the Prestons, who belong to a very old family. Miss Ismay Preston is a very accomplished girl and exceedingly clever in amateur acting. Miss Roosevelt's Wedding.—Next to her father, the President, Miss Alice Roosevelt has occupied a very large place in the estimation of the American people, and her wedding with Mr. Nicholas Longworth, which is to take place at the White House on the 17th of this month, is the one event talked about in the States. Miss Roosevelt is the only child of her father's first marriage, and is a typical American girl of some twenty Summers. Tall and slender, lint exceedingly athletic, she is good at all outdoor games, and is almost as certain with the pistol and rifle, as her father. With a full share of American free and independent notions she has decided that her wedding is to be quite a national event and on a far grander scale than any of the nine or ten weddings that have taken place at the White House. Her pre- sents make a braver show than those of any royal bride of Europe, excursion trains are to he run from all parts of the States for the wedding, and whole pages of the newspapers are given up to the event. Mr. Longwortlx is not a millionaire, but a very able lawyer and politi- cian and Americans who have the greatest admiration for the bride's strong character and personality, are confident that he will be one of the coming men in the States.

Sable Attire.—Undoubtedlv half-mourn ng effects will prevail throughout the 'London Season, and for some weeks to come black and white will be greatly in evidence. Lattr we shall have greys, lavenders, helio- tropes, and mauves. Jet trimmings are enjoying an immense vogue, and the ornaments and fringes of jet and chenille,which are finding a place on all the crepons and gauzes, are beautiful. and graceful in the extreme. It has been said, and truly, that the task of selecting becoming colours, is an easy one, compared to a wise choice in sable attire. Some women look their best in dull heavy black, others find the bright, sheeny, gauzy, varieties more becoming, while yet again there are many who find it wiser to steer a middle course. Apart from the unbecomingness of it. a wrong choice in black ages a woman considerably, therefore we should be doubly careful when sable garments are under considera- tion.

Irish Fabrics and Trimmings.—lrish poplin is in high favour among leading modistes, especially for blouses, which are worn with cloth, or cashmere skirts that match in colour exactly. For real hard wear there are few fabrics equal to a good Trish poplin, moreover it is by no means expensive, and last but by no means least. the newest colours exceed in beauty anything we have

or Accidents, uts, Burns, Bruises, Strains and Sprains

The Great Antiseptic, Price

Though in England the spirited assaults of Liberals on Tory strongholds are apt to be thought lightly of in these days of unheard-of victories, some of them are very meritorious indeed. Amongst the number should be reckoned Mr. A. M. 'Mandeville's achievement at Newark, where be fought a plucky and vigorons•fight, reducing the Tory lead to 3.28 in a constituency which it was not considered worth while contesting at the last election. Mr. Mandeville has pulled the previous majority down by no fewer than 1,963 votes. Like the typical Englishman that he is, he has already signified his intention of contesting the seat again. Mr.Nlande- ville's popularity was strongly evidenced by the fact that some 2,000 people gave him a hearty send-off when leaving Newark. He conducts" London Opinion," and is well known in London financial circles as the moving spirit of that prosperous institution "The London and Paris Exchange."

CRUEL TENPTATION.

Florence Hodgkinson,

Author of " TINSEL AND GOLD," Ec., Eic.!

CHAPTER VI.

HELEN JARDINE looked so white and ill when she reached home that her aunt was really alarmed. _

"What have von done to yourself, child ? " she asked kindly. " You were quite well when you went out, and a drive ought to do you good instead of exhausting you like this." " I was foolish," Helen said simply. " After I had done my shopping I thought I should like to go down to the sands and loOk at the sea; it brought tha; last terrible day on board the Cygnet ' back to me and somehow it made me miserable. As long as I live, Aunt Mary, I shall wish I had been the one lost and that my friend had lived." " Helen, this is positively morbid," said Lady Mary. "I am the last person to think slightingly of Aliss Jasper because she was poor, but I can't help seeing her life was a very lonely one and that in all probability death was a relief to her. Why,* Helen, even yon, who had lived with her for nearly a year, did not know the name and address of her friends. When I asked von to whom we ought to send the news of her fate you could not•tcll .

Helen had pulled herself together by an effort, and answered quietly: " I daresay aiou are right. and I am wicked. It was foolish of me to go down to the sands. I might have known that the sight of the sea would bring it all back to me." " Well, cheer up now," said Lady Mary affectionatelY. " Kenneth is coming up to dinner, and if you leak as miserable as you do now, he will think I have been beating you." "Isn't Lord Alanclale going to London for the season, or what is left of it ? " asked Helen. " No." replied Lady Maly. " Kenneth cares very little for the fashionable world, and, of course, he has all the estate business to attend to. Ever since my eldest brother died and my father's health began to fail Kenneth has acted as a sort of ' agent or steward to the Mandale property. When you come of age or marry. Helen, you will be able to make fresh arrangements, meanwhile I like to think that the place is safe under Kenneth's care." " I should never want to make any change," said Helen qeietly. • " I have often wondered what Lord Alandale meant when he spoke of being busy ; it always seemed to me he had nothing to do." " He has a great deal to do," said Lady Mary smiling; he is an artist of no mean skill, and, generally has a picture on hand. I don't mean that . is oblieed to work ' for living,' • as people put it; he may be poor for a nobleman, bat hi a mother was an heiress, and, besides Woodlands, she left him about fifteen hundred a year, quite enough to be comfortable in a quiet way." Helen had quite an hour before she must appear in the drawing-room dressed for dinner, so going to her own, room, she locked the door•end took a chair by the open window. Before the maid came to attend to her toilet she must decide one or two weighty points ; first, how much she shonlil tell the man she called Richard ; next, if she decided to pur- chase his silence how much she could spare to bribe him. The heiresa of Alainlale was not possessed Of a large allowance. • In the first days of her arrival at the .11all -Helen had felt too ill and unhappy to take much interest in anything ; her aunt had sugge:sted that she herself should choose the dresses arid other adornments, leaving Helen only the management of her pocket 'limey, which was fixed at ten pounds a month. Since she hail 'nothing to buy with it but little; peraonal matters no doubt Lady Mary considered it liberal enough. Helen had declared then she sbould never spend it, but now, in the light of Richard's demands, it seemed a very paltry sum and one which did not give much margin for hush-money. Helen Jardine had a very quick intellect and clear judgmeiit. When once she had decided that it was too late to draw back, that she could not confess her fraud to Lady Mary and the Earl. the rest was.simple enough ; she must purchase Richard's silence if it could be bought with the means at her disposal ; • if his price was too high, she must run away ; better that, better to be a miserable exile from everything good and pleasant than have to look on Lord Mandate's face and read in his honest eyes that he despised her.

She ram,' for her maid, and the girl ex- claimed at her wan, tired face. " I feel very . knocked up," Miss Jardine explained. "If I possibly can I shall go to bed directly after dinner; a long night's rest will do me good." She wore a far plainer toilet than usual thinking perhaps of her nocturnal appoint- ment, and that a long, trailing dress of net or lace would be in the way ; she had herself selected her dinner array be;L:e she rang for Susan—a soft black silk of moderate length, the bodice cut square, the elbow sleeves wide enough to disclose her pretty arm ; it was a very simple dress, but it suited Helen to perfection, though the dead black rather accentuated her pallor. The Rector of Alandale was dining at the Hall as well as Kenneth, so they were four, and not unnaturally paired off in two couples later in the drawing-room, Lady Mary and the clergyman discussing parish matters, Lord Alandale trying to rouse his cousin from the strange depression which seemed to have taken possession of her. " I think this place is too quiet for you," he said when the Rector and her aunt had sat down to chess. " Wouldn't you like to go to London for two or three weeks to be presented to the great world of the biggest city of our times ? '

" I Would much rather stay here." " Aunt Mary says you keep fretting over your friend's loss. I have been thinking, ilelen, that all the years you lived in Mel- bourne you must have made other friends besides poor Miss Jasper ; wouldn't you like to send for any of them to come over and stay with you. Voyages are so rapid now that you might get a friend here in three months." ".Oh, no ! " Thefe was no mistaking her eagerness. " I know you mean it kindly, Lord Alandale, but I should hate the very idea of it. I loved the friend! have lost very dearly, but there is no one in Australia who could take her place. Besides, if I persuaded anyone to come and she was wrecked on the voyage like I was, I should feel I had caused her death." " You are much too sensitive," said Lord Alandale quietly. "Aunt Mary is getting quite anxious about you." " She need not be ; I am perfectly well ; only the sight of the sea to-day brought everything back to me, and have cried until I have given myself a headache." " The Rector goes my way," said Lord Alandale, " and I shall make him leave in good time. for I am sure the sooner you are in bed the better." " I thought of not waiting for that," replied Helen. " I meant to slip away quietly when they were engrossed in chess." An excellent idea. I will tell your aunt your head is so bad you could not sit up amc longer." " And ask her not to trouble to come to me. I am so tired, I would much rather feel I need not speak again till to-morrow." . She slipped away unnoticed by Lady Mary, who was engrossed in an effort to avoid check- mate. Alandale looked after her with a sigh;

until to-night he really believect sne WaR getting the better of the low morbid state which had followed her trials on board the "Cygnet," but to-night he began to fear she had suffered too much ever to entirely recover her spirits. And the fear hurt him keenly. Alandale was a man of great feeling, he had begun by pitying Helen Jardine intensely. he had gone on to , taking a warm affectionate interest in her, and now, unknown, unsuspected by Lady Mary, both the pity and affection had ,given place to a warmer feeling ; he loved the heiress with his heart's best love and, he had not an idea of the truth liiinself ; it would come to him later with all the shock of a sudden surprise. And Helen? _

It was easy to dismiss Susan on the plea that she preferred to undress herself, easy to give orders that she was not to be disturbed till the morning, but the more difficult task began when she had bolted her door and was alone. . .

It was nearly ten, the West Lodge was. quite half an hour's- walk fitim_ the house ; clearly she had not much time to lose.,

She took off her bodice • and put on a long dark cloth jacket, which came down nearly to her knees ; she twisted a black lace scarf round her head and throat, then she was ready, wily she had to wait her time and opportunity ; she dared not pass through the grand hall where the butler would be on duty, she .must avoid the principal staircase alto- gether, but at the end of the long corridor on which her rooms opened was a flight of Stairs leading down to the servants' quarters, , At this hour they would most of them be gathered in their sitting-room, but there was always the danger of. meeting one of the maids in the passages. Once. „ . They were gone, and, oh, joy, they had taken the path towards the village and the Rectory, which was quite in the opposite direction to West Lodge. "Dobbs, the constable, lives next door to me; my lord," said Mrs. Martin. " Would he do for the perspn who's to hear 'me give the notice ? "

• "Admirably, and he'd be able to explain to 'Nlr. Norton that he would have to go at the ..•nd of the week."

• But when Mrs. Martin had departed Lord Alandald• felt desrately uneasy ; he did not like Norton's boast of his friendship with Helen, and a most unwelcome dosubt occurred to him. Ivy 'Cottage was the nearest house in the village to the West Lodge, of Alandale Hall, was it possible , that Helen's strange nocturnal walks were to meet the lodger who, is his landlady declared, never went out tilt 3cdtime ?

" Of course, the fellow's a scoundrel, but in liS better days he may have known Aubrey Jardine in Melbourne and rendered him scrme trifling service, on the strength of which he is trying to blaCkmail Helen. I have perfect trust in her, but I must see into this."

And his way of seeing it was a strange one ; ae explored the grounds near the 'West Lodge arefully until he found a thick cluster, of trees near enough to command the view of the approach to the great cedar beneath which the lodge-keeper had declared he saw Miss Jardine. It was summer time, and his taking a stroll before turning in for the night would excite no comment, so a little after ten Lord Alandale made his way • to his post behind the thicket, where, unseen himself, he could yet see if anyone sought, the shelter of the grand cedar. Eleven had struck and he was growing weary of his self-imposed task, when through his loop-hole of observation between the boughs he distinctly saw a man advance to- wards the cedar, look cautiously round, aml then go on to stand beneath the shelter of its 3preading bough. , . Undoubtedly it was Norton. The man answered in every way to Mrs. Martin's description of her lodger ;' he looked, still, a thorough scoundrel, though his appearance was mit so utterly revolting as it had been on the day Helen had Met him on the sands al Thornby. Since then large sums of money had passed through his hands and he had spent a few pounds on improving his attire ; he wa dressed as a gentleman now, wore collar, cuffs and shirt shirt front of snowy whiteness instead of iomorin those additions to the toilet ; thee his Flair had grown a little and had been well. groomed ; in short, looking at the figure a 5. critically as he could in the' minute at tu.l disposal and the uncertain light, Lord Alan. dale decided that in the far-off past ln might have occupied a decent position in society,. that long ago in Melbourne lii kinsman, Aubrey Jardine, might have called him a friend.

Another delay. Alandale's heart beat cc loudly he could hear its throbs. Would she. come ? Could it be that Helen Jardine would really leave her home at nearly midnight tc meet this stranger ? A footstep, and he strained his eyes tc catch sight of the advancing form, a Mende: figure dressed from head to foot in black with something between a veil and scarf wrapped round her head and half obscuring her face. _ _ .

Was it Helen ? For the life of him Lord Alandale could not have told, but then quite suddenly the doubt was ended, for he heard her voi9e "I have given you all the money I have in the World," she was saying sadly, " the brooch I have brought you to-night is the most valuable trinket I possess--I shall not have another shilling for a month. Why won't you. leave me in peace ? I novel wronged you, why won't you go away instead of stayii,g here to torture me ? "

The m-an's answer was inaudible and - when Helen spoke again her 'ioice had sunk to a lower key and Kenneth could not distiaguisL her words. He waited in agony ; each moment seemed to him an hour ; he longed to rush forward and stand between .his cousin and the man whc tortured • her, he longed to be at Heloti's sidt to fight her battles, but he would not bring on her, the humiliation )f finding herself (its. covered in Norton's company. What Nti as th:,! tie between them ? Of one thing Alandale would have staked his oath—they were not lovers. There, alone in the summer night, with that strange interview taking place only 'a iew yards off, a wonderful ddcovery came to the youne Earl. It. was this—his feeling for Helen' Jardine was not pity, nor even cousinly affec• tion, no, this girl whose whole past was a mystery to him was the love of his -life ; h€ loved her so passionately that he simply longed to break upon that tae-a-tete beneatt the cedar tree, send Norton about his busines and claim Helen as his own.

Patience—surely that was a farewell, the two whose meeting he had in a sense assisted at were surely parting. Another minute and the man came out in the open and struck along the public path which led in the direction of the village. But there was still no sign of Helen. Alandale waited till Norton was out of sight, then he strode across to the grass towards the cedar tree. Good heavens I What had happened? Helen Jardine lay stretched upon the turf;;', her sweet eyes closed, her face pale and still as though death had come to her re!: and saved her all further suffering from the man she feared

Warrington v. Millom.

After their fine win at Salford Warrington were wel- eomed at Wilderspool on Saturday by their supporters. Millom were the visitors, and considering the unimport- ance of the match, a crowd of 3,000 was satisfactory. Warrington played Lees of Chadderton at half back, for the first time. Millom were only moderately represented and Warrington had an easy win. Teatas : Warrington—Berry, back ; Kenyon, Taylor, Jones, and Harris, threequarter backs ; Lloyd and Lees, half backs; Preston, Boardman, Shugars, Heath, Harmer, Belton, Naylor, and Thomas, forwards. Millom—Syers, back; Woodburn, Gandy, Jackson, and Shelton, three-quarter backs ; Richardson and Robinson, ha If backs; Clampet, J. Richardson, Charlton, Mellor, J. Shelton, Stamper, James. and W. Richardson, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. H. Smith, Widnes. Warrington had the advantage of a strong breeze, and were early aggressive, a fine movement initiated by Lees ending in Jones scoring. Minoru could not improve matters, and after Lloyd and Taylor had been held up, Harris made the way clear for Preston to score. Faulty work by Berry let in Jackson, and Gandy placed a goal. The locals quickly got on the attack, and Harmer raced over, and also landed a4nal. Preston next raced in from loose work, and Warrington led at half-time by 19 points to 5. Turning round, Millom, although they had the advan- tage of the breeze, failed to make headway. Preston early on scored his third try, and Harmer landed the goal, and before the finish Thomas scored, and also added the goal. Result :—Warrington 4 goals and 7 tries (29 points), Millom 1 goal and 1 try (5 points).

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

HOWARD, FROM PIIILADELPHIA.-At Runcorn, on Monday, Alfred Carter' alias John Howard, was charged with attempting to rob the till of the Crown Vaults, Heath-road. it appeared that at 9 a.m. on Saturday prisoner entered the vaults and ordered a glass of beer, which was supplied by Miss Hughes, daughter of the licensee, and he was left alone. Miss Hughes heard the till door opened and shut, and returning into the bar saw the prisoner coming from behind the counter. He slid he had been for a match. Subsequently Police- constable Wilding apprehended the prisoner at Frod- sham. He gave the name of John Howard, of Phila- delphia, and said he had only been in England twelve months.—Police-constables Hulse and M‘Kenna after- _ _ _ .

wards identified him as Carter, a native of Middlewich, where he had been convicted of theft in 1867, and last

year charged with a till robbery there.—The prisoner was sentenced to seven days' bard labour.

Unduly Rrevalent Cases of Bad Temper. At the Borough Police Calla on Tuesday Juliana Turner, wife of John Turner, 29, Kerfoot-street, was

charged with attempting to commit suicido on Satur- day. The Chief Constable said it was one of those

_ cases which were gettin,g unduly prevalent of late ; it was nothing more than bad temper. On Saturday the woman was drunk, and when her husband remonstrated with her she became violent, and got hold of a la rge carving knife. The husband took no notice, and eventually she drew it across her throat. The police were sent for and she was conveyed to the Infirmary. He asked for a remand for a week, so that the state of her mind might be inquired into. The remand was :ranted.

Herbert Beard, while being taken by train to New The dressy Americans at Cairo are very dis- port, Isle of Wight, on Wednesday, eluded the police appointed with Princess Louise because she and jumped out of the carriage in a tunnel. Ile was wears a plain flannel shirt, and short skirt, and found little the worse for his foolhardy leap. tweed toque, at luncheon, and in the evening quite an ordinary gown. It will be a long time, remarks a Globe writer, before Americans I understand that Englishwomen of rank and ; first-rate breeiing like to look as inconspicuous as possible when staying at hotels. It is also difficult for . them to understand that the ! majority of well-bred British vicmen do not ; care for dressing up except for special occasions. They take their active part in life, and are real companions to their menkind. The role of the strictly ornamental they are content to leave to ' French and American women and the minority of their own nation who have the dress craze and appreciative husbands. Princess Louise and ; her sisters have never been smart or fashion- able dressers: but they always look nice and die- ; tinguished. Love of display enters little into an Englishwoman's idea of clothes. She likes to ibe smart for smart occasions, but troubles little about her dress for the ordinary ways of life. Clean, neat, and comfortable are the things that appear to matter most.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

FIRST AND FOREMOST. Cadbury's cocoa conies first among food beverages because it is very closely allied to null.: in its composition. There is really little to choose between Cadbury's absolutely pure cocoa essence and milk.—lt is highly nourish- ing, and as a daily beverage for growing children and adults at all periods of life it is unexcelled. Cocoa prepared in the Cadbury way is rich and in the highest degree strengthening and invig- orating, yet so refined as to be digested with ease by the most delicate stomach.

CASSEROLE DE BCEUF AITX CH A M PIGEONS. —lt is ' a little difficult to improve that homely dish ; stewed steak at a moment's notice, but it can be done, writes Mrs. Alfred Praga in the Lady. Fry some rolls of very thinly-cut bacon in the usual way. Dish up and reserve. Take the con- tents of half a bottle (not tin) of button mush- rooms. Drain. Rinse in hot water. Dry gently. Fry for five or six minutes in the bacon fat. Make a brown casserole very hot. Dish up the steak (it should be cut into neat strips) in this. Garnish with the carrots and onions, Sm. Then put the rolls of bacon and the fried mush- rooms on top. Serve as a "casserole de brut aux champignons." It will sound, look, and also taste infinitely better. The Examiner, Saturday, February 10, 1906. WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASIIION-110ME.

SPANISH LACE /N VOGUE.

The return of black Spanish lace to favour is one of the most important features of to-day. Those who are in possession of long Spanish shawls worn in the crinoline days, and which were draped lightly round the shoulders, and fell in a well-defined point behind, are having them reconverted and renewed for the coming summer days, when they will form an effective supple- ment to many of the gowns in light seasonable fabrics. The Second Empire shawl-bodice, which will make its appearance in many forms this season, will give an incentive to an already grow- ing fashion, and the white or cream mantilla in fine gossamer silk will constitute the chief adorn- ment of the corsage. Long Spanish scarves in ivory-white will also be much worn to throw over the shoulders for evening and theatre wear, while as an accompaniment to afternoon gowns of silk muslin they promise to in great measure supersede the scarf of mousseline de soie or net.

===== 0002601_19060210_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
MATRON'S AFTERNOON GOWN.

An afternoon gown for a matron is shewn in our illustration. It could be carried out in a rich shade of prune-coloured cloth, with trimmings of knife-pleated frilling to match. The bodice opens over a vest of closely-gathered mimosa lace, and crosses over slightly at the waist, where it is finished with a folded belt of prune-coloured silk, the two rounded straps over the shoulders giving

the effect of a collar; they are stitched at th edges and trimmed with the pleated frilling. The sleeves are puffed high up on the arm, and are drawn in above the elbow with a curved stiap of cloth edged with frilling. The under.sleeves are composed of the mimosa lace, arranged in short puff, and finishing in a full frill. The skirt has a narrow panel in front and inverted pleats behind, and is trimmed about the hem with bands of cloth edged with the pleated silk,

GOSSIP.

Madame Carlotta Ide, a new soprano who fis already making a name at London concerts, made her first appearance at the Crystal Palace the other evening, when she sang before a large audience at the promenade concert, and scored an immediate success, being enthusiastically en cored for her rendering of Arditi's vocal waltz "L'Estasi." During her absence in India, the Princess of Wales does not forget English friends. Mrs. Hodder, the manager of the Church Army Prisoners' Wives Aid Department, has just re- reived an order from the Princess for making ninety shirts, the work to be carried out by the Work Guild which has been formed among the wives of men undergoing imprisonment.

A SMART OVERALL.

For those who study economy, the art of tak- ing care of one's clothes is so necessary that we are giving a sketch of an overall, which will pro- tect the dress whilst cooking or doing other household duties, and is also attractive enough In meet the requirements of any girl who is en- gaged in an artistic pursuit. For the latter we would suggest a dark-green or blue art linen, em-

broidered in lighter toned flax thread; but for home wear holland or brown linen is the most suitable material. Our design is made with a plain yoke, which is cut out to a V at the neck. The largo collar over the shoulders has its cor- ners embroidered with a largo open pattern in red or blue thread. The "skirts," which are made all in one, are gathered into the yoke, and drawn in at the waist with a pointed belt, em- broidered in front, and finishing behind in long strings, which are tied in a bow. CHILL ON THE STOMACH.

Bile Beans Work a Wonderful Cure.

The dangerous after-effects of a severe cold are strikingly illustrated in the case of Mrs. G. Shearer' of 9, Hull Terrace, Crown-road, Leeds. She was a healthy, vigorous woman until she caught a severe cold, which took the form of gastric catarrh. Ulcers formed in her stomach, and in a few short weeks her health suffered so severely that she had to take to her bed, where she remained for a month. Describing her sufferings and her cure by Bile Beans, Mrs. Shearer says "For seven weeks I was very ill. I had pains at the pit of the stomach after everything I ate. These pains eventually became so acute that I dare not eat any solid food. After a meal, however light, I was dreadfully sick; in fact, I could get nothing to stay on my stomach. Acute headaches attacked me constantly, and my chest and throat became very sore because of the continual retching. At last the suffering got so bad that I had to take to my bed, where I was kept for a month. A doctor attended me, and I took his medicines, but received very little benefit. "Hearing of the good Bile Beans were doing, I decided to give them a trial. I obtained benefit from the first box, so I decided to undergo a thorough conrEe. The bickness and vomiting attacks ceased; my chest and throat grew stronger, and I was again able to eat and enjoy a good meal without fear of after-effects. I am now completely recovered from m 7 illness, and never have an attack of indigestion. Bile Beans have set me up in a sound, healthy condition." Mrs. Shearer's testimony affords a typical instancesof the efficacy of Bile Beans as a restorative tonic after serious illness. Bile Beans stayed the ravages of gastric catarrh, healed the ulcers in the stomach, restored and strengthened the functions of digestion, and thus laid the foundation of robust health. Bile Beans owe their great success to the fact that they use Nature's own weapons to help all the organs of the body to workout their own salvation. Not so with cheap substitutes, which make a cure more difficult to secure when the system has been filled with their coarse, nauseous ingredients. THE LIFE STORY OF TWO SLUM CHILDREN,

THE LADY HENRY SOMERSET.

They looked after each other. There was Dc one else to look after them, for their father was in the infirmory, mad their mother was rarely sober. So Mewl ie comforted Georgie when he was in trouble, tual in return Georgie stood up for Maudie and protected her when the bigger girls of the court attempted to bully her He mind- ed " Biby," while she came to the Baud of Hope, and she minded " Biby " while he went off with the other boys ; but as a rule they trotted about together —an odd pair. .He was tall for his age, and a young scamp he looked with his broad happy face, and a grin that stretched from ear to ear, showing off • his big white teeth. His hair was clipped close to his skull, and his ears stood out from his head like handl& ; his clothes—that is to say, his shirt and trousers—were quite three sizes too small for him. As he explained himself, " they fits inc too soon, sister.' The shirt collar was tight, so it was seldom buttoned, but flapped back. leaving his white chest bare ; his trousers ended ill a fringe at the knees. Maudie was small. ller face was white and thin—a very common type of it slum child—an old, worn, anxious face, with wrinkles already about her witchy dark eyes. Iler gown, it one can dignify that, gar- ment by such a name, consisted of a sleeveless bodice, and at skirt , which dragged at her heels. A rusty black cape stretched across her Darrow shoulders, succeeded in covering her arms at least to her elbows; a pair of boots already well worn by her mother, completed her cos- tume. Neither of theni had ever known the luxury of a bat when I first knew them, but one day shortly after 1 made their acquaintance, a friend sent me a box full of old hats. I gave Maudie one, as broad-brimmed white one, with a frill of white lace, and a wreath of poppies on it. She spent that day admiring herself in the shop windows. till Georgie, who despised a hat, said he'd play by himself, " sooner nor pliy with a girl that carn't pars a winder wi'ont a- lookin' at herself." Then Moodie took the hat indoors, and was never again known to wear it on a week•day, but on Sundays she might be seen triumphantly parading the Court in all the glory of her treasured finery. In summer I used to meet them going off to I,legent's Park for a long day's enjoyment, carrying the baby turn about. While. baby conveniently slept un- der a tree they anmeed themselves by turning cat in the wheel or going head over heels in the soft green grass. Maudie was not so accom- plished as Georgie ; her legs would not stand up so straight in the air as his did, neither could she stand so long on her head as lie could ; but that may have been owing to the fact that Georgie's head was bigger and flatter than lifers was. This was the. reason she gave, and Georgie ;accepted it with becoming pride. H the day was vesy hot, they sat by the lake aud dabbled their dusty legs in the cool waiter. They tried one day to make "liby " appreciate the refreshing coldness of the waster, but " Eiby " loudly objected. Indeed she "yelled so dread- ful that it copper coined along an' gived its a good jawring ; he said what we was a tormiht- in' the child." One never-to-be-forgotten day they went for a ride on the top of it bus " all the wiy to 'Yde Park Corner." Coming back it fellow passenger gave them a penny. George gat off and bought 43owe apples. He had to run very hard to catch up on the bus—where Maudie sat shrieking with excitement and fear—but didn't they " 'eve a good (line a-ridin' 'ome cattle them apples ?" On the whole they were happy through the summer. Mother locked them out, and went off drinking with the key hi I her pocket. But what if she didn't come home ' till early, morning ? The nights were warm, and it was very pleasant sleeping out in the court. And Mr. Green always gave them a fruit supper when he crone back from his stall. They thought it wits wonderfully kind of Mr. Green to give them the rotten fruit instead of throwing it out into the street. The doorstep at the Hall was very comforMble to sleep on, though un- fortunately there was only room for 0118 to lie on it. But they took it in turns' and the one on the step land to hold the baby—the pavement was rather chilly for baby, especially when she was teething. Maudie openly preferred the door- step and despised the • pavement. Georgie thcmght the pavement had its advantages, Ile liked to lie on his back and look up at the strip of dark sky above ; there were generally three stars to be seen, " a big 'ml an' two little ones, the little ones they winks ,at me, but the big 'tot jist looks clean at me, sister." The only drawback to those happy nights was that. mother was apt to disturb them about two in the morning. If she was very drunk she went straight to bed and forgot all about them till she was sober, when she was sure to be cross. Then she would kick Georgie and cuff Menthe's sleepy head, as she bade them come indoors, and not disgrace her by sleeping on the doorstep. The children were afraid to cry, because once when they did—they were very sleepy—one of the neighbours put, her head out of a window to, see what the row was about, and as Mrs. ,Tones was not held in high repute by the other in- habitants of the court, the said neighbour pro- ceeded to give Mrs. Jones a piece of her mind. Aren't you fit to look arter children ; call yerself a mother P Wicked, drunken old slut !" This led to a row, and the children: had to hold on to tbeir mother's skirts to prevent her from going up to " tear the hair out of that meddina' old ca's head." _

That August was very hot, and about the end of the month Mandie fell sick. Georgie nursed her with tenderest care; he carried her up to the " Buryin' Ground " in the mornings, amesat by her side under a tree through the long days. Maggie Green " minded biby " for him while Maudie was sick. He AVM worried at first because she was too ill to eat, but she enjoyed the cold water from the drinking fountain. Through the heat of the day her bend ached so badly that she could not bear him to speak, but to- wards evening when the air was cooler she liked to hear him tell all about the country. He had spent one day in Essex when his Sunday school bad a treat. Mandie lay on her back, and looked .up into the lime trees above, and imagined as Georgie talked that it really Was the country,. and not "an 'ole buryin' ground." They stayed till the cemetery was closed, and then Georgie carried her back to the court. When she got better she was more devoted than ever to Georgie. She followed about at his heels when he went playing with the other boys, and tore pieces off her gown to mend the holes in his trousers. She got stronger as the cold weather came in, and by December she was quite well again. Then Georgia's eyes quite lost the seri- ous, wistful look they had worn since the day she fell sick. Their great delight was to trot off to Tottenham Court Road, and stand by Shoolhred's. to watch the carriages drive up, and the " grand lydies " step out. The foot- men, too, with their furs, were much admired, and the windows with the lovely fairy dresses. When it was dark and the " grand lydies " bad gone home, they came back to the court, and sat on a step, and dreamed they were " grand lydies what can buy fairy dresses an' ave good blow outs at cawfee shops." This was the only happiness they had through the cold, bitter weather, for mother was drinking harder than ever, and there was no food in the house. _ _

One day " sister" rigged triem out in thick warm clothes ; but mother said the old ones would do them nicely, and took the new . clothes off to the pawn shop. But even then, when the wind blew through and through their clothes, they were anything tut miserable. They played at cat in the wheel to warm themselves, or trotted up and down the street " lookin' in the winders, and a-smellin' the fried fish." One day, us they sat together on a step, enjoy- ing a roast " pertater," chuckling softly with delight over this unusual luxury, a " copper " came lip, and before Maudie could speak, (leorgie was gone. She sat still, the " per- ratcr. " cold and forgotten in her hand : she did not cry, but bee breath came hard and fast, and something hurt in her chest. *Late that. even- ing, I found her sitting on our step ; she was soaked through by the cold, wet sleet. I tried : to talk to her, to comfort her, but the calm des- pair of the child was beyond Inc. I felt that her 1 Sorrow was too deep to be easily understood. ' For the next week It weary-faced little ghost : haunted the court. Mother went, before the magistrate—in her best bonnet, taken out of pawn for the occasion —and wept it little, and said that Georgie had always been a bad boy. It is not lair, T thought, that, 60 many women are light-liearted and free from' care, that so many are shel- tered and protected from everything that might sadden them. and I have to bear burdens of my own and other people's as well. .Inst then, a flash, I saw a neat dormitory with neat beds down each side. In one bed a big lad lay, his 'face buried ill his pillow, sobbing his heart out, and with Georgie's eyes I saw a little girl, sit- ting on the floor by floe . window in a dark room. And the light of those stirs shone on the face of a child with a broken he irt.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Methodist Anniversary.

Anniversary services in connection with the Legh- street Primitive Methodist Church, were held on Sunday. The Rev..Tohn Whittle, of Buxton, was the special preacher for the day, and he addressed large congrega- tions at both morning and evening services. In the afternoon Mr. Guest presided over a musical service, when Miss A. Griffiths (soprano), sang "The sailor's grave "and " There is a green hill far away "Miss L. Plinston (contralto), " The lost chord" and" Into Thy hands ." Mr. W. Warburton (tenor), "Behold I stand " and" Calvary ; " Mr. W. A. Clay! "Sun of my soul" and "Ora pro nobis." The choir rendered several anthems. Mr. Fred Podmore presided at the organ, and Mr. A. Spruce acted as the conductor.

===== 0002601_19060210_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
Weslayanism in Warrington

During the last month, says "The Warrington Circuit Wesleyan Methodist Church Record," many of our meetings have been interrupted by the excitement attendant upon the election. This we do not regret,as it was our earnestdesire that our people should take their full part in the historic national crisis through which we have passed. We are heartily glad that they have done so. Now, however, it is of the utmost importance that we should rally to our church work in all its depart- ments with all possible vigour. There are signs of encouragment on all hands. We have increasing con- gregations, overflowing Sunday schools, and not a few conversions are taking place. The winter season, which affords our best opportunity for advancement, is quickly passing. Let everyone be in his place, and whatsoever our hand fludeth to do, let us do it with our might.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was again a very goodgathering at this meet- ing on Sunday last. The hon. secretary Mr. B. Farrar presided and read the thanks for votes of condolence re- ceivedifroml friends who had recently lbeen bereaved, and proposed a vote of sympathy with the family of the late Mrs. Joseph Knowles.He also urged the brethren to aggressive work for the Master. Mr. C. F. Fooks, Manchester, spoke upon "The lessons, from a bicycle, its wheel, rim, spokes, tyre' brake and light," in a most interesting manner. Its wheel as to run, its rim the committee the spokes its members, the tyre to run easily over the difficulties of life; the brake to pull up before the temptations of life; the light the word of God to light up our path and show us the dangers around us. Mr. Joseph Garner and Mr. G. Smiley also took part in the meeting. One new member was heartily welcomed, making a total of 542. The Secretary announced the open Sunday next Sunday which would be a big day. The Apollo Choirunder the leadership of Mr. Harry Berry will contribute to the musical programme.

Though Free Trade has THE REVIVAL OF triumphed on so decisive a PROTECTIONISM. scale in the late elections, there is another fact which is quite as noteworthy, and that is that we have once more in the House of Commons an undis- guised Protectionist party. This is a new feature in our political life. Protection has been abandoned as a policy for half-a-century past—abandoned, we mean, not only by the nation, bat as a party rally- ing cry. The old standard was lowered, and then thrown aside, because nobody would gather round it. There have always been Protectionists in the country and in the House of Commons; but they were solitary persons. In Parliament there were never more than two or three at a time, and they were seldom closely associated. Mr. HENRY CHAPLIN and Sir HOWARD VINCENT were like deep calling unto deep. Perhaps they each felt, like DAVID DEAN. , some doubt of the other's fitness to belong to the true church.

What it is important to consider is that in the case of these gentlemen Protectionist dectrines were regarded as a fat and that they are now openly adopted as the principles of by much the largest section of the Conservative party in the j new House of Commons. Mr. DISRAELI, the last Protectionist Leader, said that Protection was not only dead but done for in a quite emphatic and final Sense. JOHN BRIGHT knew better. He foresaw that the old battle for Free Trade would have to be fough t over and over again. Experience leads to the conclusion that Protection, like the Eastern Question, reasserts itself at intervals of about twenty-five- years. It will present itself in a fresh guise, and under a new name, to every generation. The entry of the Conservatives into power put an end to the Pro- tectionist agitation which preceded the Tory victory of 1886. Ostensibly it was an agitation for Free Trade, but it differed in no particular from tho new Tariff Reform or the old Pro- tection. It had to be met by the same argu- ments and the same demonstrations of its futility. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN was a mighty Free Trader in those days. He not merely rode down Mr. FARRAR BOR3I'D, but trampled upon him. The speeches delivered in and about 1885 by the present raging and tearing campaigner are the best speeches that have been delivered on behalf of Free Trade since the death of Cobden. They covered the whole field so completely that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has merely had to invert thorn in order to discover a fully-equipped Tariff Reform policy. Each of his present arguments is answered by anticipation in one of his speeches of more than 1 twenty yeats ago. It seemed at that time as if Protection might be taken up by the Conservative leaders. Sir STAFFORD NORTHCOTE spoke of Free Trade as a "pious opinion." Lord SALISBURY used language of a doubtful character. Lard RANDOLPH CHURCHILL was little short of an out-and out Protectionist. But the subject was immedi- ately dropped when the Conservatives came into power. It had possessed a certain electioneering value; but it was incontinently laid aside when it had served its purpose. Years afterwards, when Mr. CHAPLIN became a Member of the Cabinet, he was expected as a preliminary to make something like a formal recantation of his Protectionist views. As a compensatory fad, we remember, he took up with bimetallism. Protec- tionist views were thou much too unfashionable for Cabinets, In this respect there has been a great and ominous change. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has made a disastrous mistake as regards the country at large; hut he has again raised Protectionism to the dignity of a party issue. The Member of Parliament who believes in it is no louger regarded as a person with a bee in his bonnet. He must be treated as a serious politician. In the new House of Commons be will find a hundred others holding the same views as himself. This is the new and grave element of the situation. The Protectionist battle will not now have to be fought by stealth. Once again Protection is a policy with a strong backing. It has been so badly beaten of late that there is probably nothing to be feared from it for another twenty or twenty-five years; but it will continue throughout that time not only to shew a bold front, but to worm its way underneath. The , duty of Free Traders, therefore, must be eternal vigilance. They are engaged in a conflict Welch will never really come to an end ; and it is a con- flict for the preservation of the foundations of our ational prosperity and for the people's bread.

The aldermanic election at ALDERMANIC Tuesday's Council meeting ELECTIoN, was significant in one res- pect— that it marked the end of promotion by seniority, which hes been the guiding principle for several years. We regret that Councillor Luke Ellison failed to obtain the majority of the votes, for he is the senior Conn- cillor and in other respects had as strong a claim las Councillor—novv Alderman—John Roberts. Councillor Ellison lost because he was not accept- able to the Non-Traders, who in matters of this kind now have the deciding voice. They care little for seniority, wherein we differ from them. Seniority does, certainly, give a man some claim to the respect of his fellows and it is impossible not to take it into consideration. Whether it should be the determining factor in an alderruanic election is, however, an open question. There are strong arguments bath for and against, but on the side of seniority there is one incontestable reason —it does away with party strife. For the future, we suppose the old order of things will prevail, and the various parties in the Council—now numbering three—will fight over the alderrnanic bone of contention. By and by there may be a fourth party—Lab mr—with a voice which cannot be ignored, arid it too will want its aldermen. Apparently the man who looks for future promotion will have to be " all things to all noon," and a bit more tp the party with the strongest backing.

We are quoting here an

"MoNKEYIKa" expression- used by the WITH THE L.G.R Mayor on Tuesday, when

he alluded to the 'action ! of the Local Government Board in deduct- ing over • £l,OOO from the sum the Corpora- tion seeks to borrow to complete the new police premises, and in reducing the period for repay- ment of the loan by two years. The phrase is an excellent one, and describes with remarkable accuracy the line of conduct which has , ruled in Warrington with respect to cor- porate dealings with the Board. We were, however, somewhat surprised to hear it Come from th,e lips of the Mayor—it sounds more like a Non-Trading criticism of municipal policy. Possibly it indicates a measure of very desirable repentance. The Corporation has "monkeyed " with the Local Government Board too long already, and it were well that a different attitude should be adopted in all future dealings with an authority possessing such absolute powers and governed by so thoroughgoing a municipal reformer as the Right Hon. ''John Burns, M.P. Knuckle-rapping is apt to become very painful if frequently repeated. The Mayor ' conveyed a distinctly wrong impression in the course of his remarks, however. Anyone reading his speech would imagine that the period of repay- ment for this loan in particular had been reduced from 50 years to 28 years, and that this, combined with the deduction from the sum required, would involve a call upon the rates of 1-id. in the This is not the case. Fifty years would not he granted in any circumstances for such a loan as the one in question, and the reduction of the period of repayment inflicted by the Board only , amounts to two years. The call upon the rates, therefore,does not amount to any such sum at 11d, in the. Though His Worship was wrong in the impression he conveyed, his warning was a very proper one and we hope he will back it up by taking a personal interest in seeing that the Corporation henceforth conforms strictly to Local Government Board regulations, and abandons the foolish atm dangerous game of" monkeying."

The tree-planting mania TREE PLANTING. at Stockton Heath has spread like chicken-pox or the measles, and in a few years it will be almost impossible to " see wood for trees." We have full sympathy with tree-planting and town beautifying when judiciously carried out, but the tree-planters of Stockton Reath seem to have run riot. It wits only the other day that we took a stroll through the village for the first time since the tree-planters had their way, and we were amazed at the fruit of their labours. Trees, trees, everywhere, and worse to avoid than the citizens of a crowded city. The ! tree-planters have overdone their part. Beautifica- tion of the thoroughfares is eminently 'Raise- worthy, but at first glance one would imagine ' that the sole aim of the community in ' this important and increasing district was to grow trees. Shrubs stand cheek by jowl and well- nigh jostle each other—while they unmistakably jostle the pedestrian. The fact of the matter is ! obvious,that most of the footpaths are too narrow to accommodate trees, iron hurdles, and ordinary "humans " at the same time, and as the feat- paths were primarily intended for the use of the latter, the two former will eventually have to give way. It is no Wonder that a strong letter of complaint from a -resident was read at the Rural Council meeting on Tuesday,though the aggrieved person was rude in calling the tree-planters "faddists." The fad is an admirable one, but it has been over-indulged, and as a result of the Rural. Council's decision the reiponsibdity wiits thrown upon the Parochitil Committee of finding the happy medium between a reasonable public improvement an an unnecessary obstruction.

Whenever some particularly CYCLDTS AND deserving charital.ile under- CHARITY. taking has stood in urgent need of support, the War- rington cyclists have been amongst the first to respond to the appeal. It has beet! a real pleasure to them to organize fancy dross parades which have not only provided the public with elaborate cpectacular displays, but have also been the means of raising considerable sums for the most worthy institutions. We hear that a suggestion has been made that a pAracle be organized this year on behalf of the Warrington Infirmary extension fund. Of course, it is too early as yet to say anything definite beyond the fact that the committee of the Cycling Club favour the proposal and, that the 'earliest opportunity will be taken to ascertain the feeling of the general body of members. The matter is down for discussion at the annual meet- ing next Thursday and we have little doubt that it will be sympathetically received. Whatever may be the ultimate result, it certainly indicates great public spirit on the part of the leaders of the club that they have so early shown a willingness to consider the claims of this scheme—one of the most pressing which has to be faced. The cyclists could not devote their energies to a better cause, for the accommodation at the Infirmary has been so severely taxed of late that an extension has become imperative. If a parade is organised, we believe that adequate public support will be a fore gone conclusion.

Some interesting figures ADVERTISEMENT were given at the dinner of :EN POLITICS. the Sphinx Club this week as to the amount of political advertising that was done in the General Election. The limit as to a candidate's expensu has affected the patronage given to the newspapers; but posters and leaflets have flourished as they have never flourished before. We affect in these days to smile at the evangelical tracts which were such a feature of religious life in bygone days; but the tract has proved itself a reviving institution—the difference has been that it has been circulated for the gospel of Free Trade or the evangel of Mr. Chamberlain. _ph, use of political pictures on hoardings is, however, a modern affair. It is the application to the democracy of the satire which toe caricaturistiof old devoted formerly to the seleo. Our caricatures at the present time, however, are not nearly so bitter as wore those of a century ago, and the subjects are more discreetly chosen. Royalty and high society were the favounto objects of ridicule, and the ridicule was often exceedingly rude. Now conviion is sought by raising a good.huinoured laugh, The most satisfactory thing, too, is that the victim of personal caricature is, as often as not, when it is done good-humouredly, flattered in his own mind at the notice he gets, and so while the purpose is n served everyone is pleased. It has bee one of the greatest handicaps of the unionist party in recent conflicts that they have had no caricaturist of tie calibre of "FOG.' to wage war on their behalf. It is an asset that money cannot secure, for talent of this sort seems to be born end not made.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

According to the Mayor the Ratepayers and Property Owners Association "cannot see a hole through a ladder."

* * No reference was made at the Council meeting on Tuesday to the report by Mr. 'Clarside, C.E., on the Lawson contract.

* * * Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., replied to his slanderous detractors in very lively fashion on Monday evening at Warrington, but he might with advantage and dignity have used more*" Parliamentary "language.

Mr. Maurice Wilson, president of the Society of Engineers, speaking in London on Monday night,said that he hoped that it was not improbable that the Manchester Ship Canal would eventually be continued across England to Hull. William Martinscroft*, *who has just 'led in the Prestwich Asylum after being chargeable to the Warrington Union for fifty-years was the oldest asylum inmate in Lancashire. His maintenance had cost the ratepayers £1,300. *

Can you imagine red cabbage not only pickled but in a state of extreme nervous tension with every strip curled up as if in agony ? If so you will have a very fair idea of the latest novelties in straw utilised in the brims of new toques.—The " Tatler." * * * The Flowing Tide seems to have extended even to the football field. We read that "- England's Rugby team to oppose Ireland has been chosen.. Radical changes have been made in the three-quarter line." And yet the men who chose the team were Rugby Unionists.—" Globe." _

On January 30th Alder Man Tinnion (chairman) and the members of the Paving and Sewerage Committee sat for 41 hours with a short interval fortes, on coin- mitiee business. Even then they had to leave several important matters over for a subsequent meeting. The position of a Town Councillor is not all gilt and glory.

I

* * The Museum and Library Committee acknowledges the receipt of " Parliamentary papers from Mr. It. Pierpoint AT.P."- This is a line that has periodically appeared in the Committee's minutes for many years, but" the old order changeth and giveth place to the new." If the committee values these papers it had better apply to a fresh source for a continuation of the supply. * * * "The principle on which this Council has carried out its work for a long time has been to do a thing slowly, and do it badly." This pithy declaration by Councillar Maginn delighted the opposition " in the Town Council on Tuesday. Alderman Smethurst had begun to applaud at the word " slowly," but he apparently expected a different conclusion to the sentence.

* * * There was more than one" bull "in the course of Tuesday's lively Council meeting, but the best bit of unconscious humour came from Councillor Wilkinson, who announced with emphasis : " Warrington is the town of my birth, and 1 am proud to say not of my adoption." After the rude guffaws of the multitude had subsided, Councillor Stott remarked that he too was "proud of the town of his birth, but unlike Councillor Wilkinson he had had no choice in the matter." * * *

An interesting question was put by Councillor Burton to Alderman Evans, chairman of the Sanitary Works Committee. The Councillor asked what heat was 'required to deodorise the gases from Longford Depot. The Alderman looked momentarily puzzled and then replied that it_was sufficient to pass them through the combustion chamber of the destructor. But no one is any wiser as to the heat required. If the heated language of the inhabitants of Longford, when they are blessed with a sou' west wind, does not deodorise the gases then we should think no heat above the earth's crust would be effective. The gases are of a very strong and stubborn quality. * * The electorate will welcome the announcement made in the " Liberal Magazine " that all public directorships held by members of the present Govern- ment have to be given up by ministers on their acceptance of office. It is pointed out that this very salutary rule, observed in the Liberal Government which went out in 1895, was discarded by Lord Salis- bury and his successor, Mr. Balfour. The Liberal party has constantly protested against " the Minister director," and instances have not been wanting in-the last ten years of the extreme desirability (to use no harsher word) of Ministers having been engaged in the direction of public companies. Sir E. Campbell- Bannerman has performed a very real public service by his revival of a rule which, once adopted, ought never to have been abandoned. *4* There has just been withdrawn from active service by the London and North-Western Railway another world-famous locomotive, this being Trevitbick's Bft. Gin. " single " Cornwall, which has done duty for nearly sixty years. An interesting accoutit of the engine_is given in this month's " Railway Magazine," which states that. she was built at Crewe in 1847 from the designs of Mr. F. Trevithick, ECM of the famous " Father of the Locomotive." No actual record of the mileage of the engine has been preserved, but there is little doubt that it reaches well over a million miles. We can well remember that, in our sclmol-days, we often used to gaze in awe at the gigantic 81t. Gin. driving wheel of the old "Cornwall" as she per- formed her daily task on the low level line from Man- chester to Warrington and Liverpool. No engine could compare with her for exercising a fascination on the youthful mind. *4* We have received a letter from the Secretary of the National Anti-Vaccination League which is more than a little amusing. The kssociation takes credit for the defeat of Mr. Henry Chaplin in Sleaford Division because the victor is a member of the Council of the Association. We had thought that the great free food question had brought about the down- fall of that High Priest of Protection. In Darwen Division " neither candidate satitifiqd the anti-vaceina- ird te i joft tors," and it is claimed that the latter held the election in their hands because the majority was only 25. We do nW,suppose anyone in Darwen believes, hoivevei, that a candidate for the constituency has only to pro- fess anti-vaccinnation principles to secure election. A very interesting programme of resolutions fore- casting Labour policy in the new Parliament has been arranged for discussion at the L.R.C. meeting in London next week. The resolutions aim, among other things, at clearly defining the position of trade unions, securing funds for remedying unemployment from the national exchequer, and fixing a standard minimum wage for government and municipal employees of 30s. per week. The latter is the only pro- posal of the lot which may be called extreme and if it finds support from the L.R.C. it is not likely to live ;long in the House of Commons. As an indication that 1 the L.R.C. is not putting forward just those subjects which will please the workingman it is worth while noting that it is proposed to obtain the prohibition of the 'publication of all betting news. This is a level- headed attempt to protect the workers against them- selves. What Itith these matters, together with taxa- tion reform, extension of the franchise to women, free meals and free education, the Labour policy bids fair to secure substantial support in the people's Parlia- ment of to-day.

* * * With regard to the new street question a corres- pondent writes : " The game of shuttlecock "has in Mr. Alderman Tinnion's opinion been played over the matter. It is an apt description, for the number of resolutions and amendments that the Corporation have passed in connection with it is not only surprising but lamentable. This is not a busmesa way of dealing with it and it is not at all surprising that, already, through altered plans and interference with building erection, compensation has had to be paid. For example, as was disclosed at the Council meeting„over £OOO had to be paid to Messrs. Hodgkinson. Even Local Government Board inquiries cost money, so of course, we presume that if Mr. Lord's motion is put in force, then further expense has been needlessly incurred. There are two sides to this matter. Some are advocating the practical stoppage of any further work on the street and, as they state, further expense. On the other hand there are advoc ites who consider it folly now that the street is nearing completion, to place any hindrance in the way, and do not hesitate to describe it as"false economy." Mr. Councillor Magian and Alderman Bennett were strong on this point. Whatever be the outcome, the Corporation has not in the matter, added to its reputation for businesi capacity:"

MR. J. A. SEDDON, M.P., INTERVIEWED.

Personal Charges Refuted.

THE PROSPECTS OF THE LABOUR MOVEMENT.

"Are you a gentleman ? " The question was put by an " Examiner " reporter in opening an interesting interview he obtained with ,Mr. James A. Seddon, M.P. who visited Warrington • on Monday. The Labour member for Newton Division smiled and looked surprised. On being told, however, that certain Tories in the constituency had been heard bemoaning the fact that they were no longer repre- sented by a " gentleman " he immediately replied: "If to be a gentleman is to make no disclaimer to , scurrilous charges, knowing them to be untrue, I ' forego any title to 'gentleman.' But if to be a gentle- man is to fight a political campaign upon political questions, then I claim to be the only gentleman who has fought in the Newton contest." .

A long and arduous election campaign does not appear to have over-taxed Mr. Seddon's energies, and our replrter, who waited upon him at the residence of Councillor Stott, one of the most active of local Labour workers, found him as bright and vigorous as ever. He willingly consented to talk or his own career and of the position and prospects of the Labour mo.ement.

Going into auto-biography, Mr. Seddon said he was born at Prescot in 1805, his father being a collector in the service of the Local Government Board under the Prescot Board of Guardians. Having received an elementary education Mr. Seddon served an apprentice- ship to the grocery business. He worked " behind the counter " for sixteen years and then became a commercial traveller. For ten years he discharged those duties and at the present time holds a number of agencies in the commercial world. Mr. Seddon's I entrance into trade union work took place when, as a shop-worker, he was appointed to the presidency of an early closing organization in St. Helens. On the formation of the National Union of Shop Assistants he was instrumental in inducing the St. Helens organiza- tion to become affiliated with it. Since then he has attended every annual delegates' meeting,having served on the executive for several years and occupying the presidential chair in 1902. Mr. Seddon has been associated with the St. Helens and Prescot district all his life. . _

The interviewer then asked Mr. Seddon for some details as to how the great Labour triumph in the Newton division had been accomplished. "lie St. Helens" said Mr. Seddon "I was one of the pioneers in the formation of a Labour party which in four short years has returned eight local Councillors and a member of Parliament When I was asked to contest the Newton division, it seemed a very hopeless task. There were no funds and no organization, but the political character of the late representative had iso incensed the workers that they were prepared to take on the Herculean task of not only raising the money but fighting the seat in face of all the ' disadvantages existing. An active twelve months' stammer campaign in the open air gradually educated the electorate to the fact that there WAS a new force coming into tha political life of this country—a party destined at no distant date to play its part in the social amelioration of the people. The chief difference between the two candidates, recog- nized by aN, was that. one stood for the plutocrat and capital, and the other for the people and organized labour. The burning political questions had less bear- ing noon the contest here than in most of the other constituencies. The well-known fact that the late member had consistently opposed Labenr legislation had the effect of binding together the Liberal and Tory working men, and from that cause the victory was chiefly secured." Mr. Seddon next had something to say as to the methods adopted by the Conservatives during the contest, He had, he said, always given the " gentle- men's " party the credit of being sportsmen who were able to take their thrashings in a sportsmanlike spirit. He had, however, found out that not being able to attack the policy supported by the Labour candidate, and the record of the late member being so black from a Labour point of view, the Con- servatives began to make personal attacks on his (Mr. Seddon's) character, honour and career. " Charges of bankruptcy, atheism, and wife-beating were levelled against me by persons with sinister anotives who knew to the contrary," said Mr. Seddon. " I am anxiously looking out for the people who have I circulated the rumours. and if there are any in War- rington, who have the pluck, I invite them to put the charges on paner and I will give them every oppor- tunity to find out whether I have been in the Bank- ruptcy Court or whether my conduct will not stand in ' as favourable a light as the late member's or that of any other gentleman of the Tory party." " You give an unqualified denial to the charges then ?" _ .

!" breezes" but he does not anticipate that it will entail a great deal of fighting. Of course, it would be taken for granted that the Labour party would sit in opposition to any Government, but that did not necessarily mean that they would always be hostile to what the Government did, _

'Mr. Seddon believes that the first piece of construc- tive policy to be pressed forward will be the feeding of school children, and that will probably arise out of the Education Act. The Labour party holds that every child in the elementary schools should be fed, and that those parents who are able to pay should do so. There must, however, be no classification which would suggest pauperization. Mr. Seddon looks upon this as one of the most important problems confront ing the House of Commons, as he considers it utter folly to try and teach a child who is suffering the pangs of hunger. "So you think the outlook is bright ?" asked the reporter in conclusion. " Yes. Not so much from the success of the present moment, but because there is a new spirit entering into , the political life of the country, and men of all classes are beginning to recognize that we shall have to have more of the Golden Rule and less of the law of the jungle in our commercial and national life." Mrs. Seddon had accompanied her husband on his visit to Warrington and was full of enthusiasm on the attainment of such a great victory for Labour in the Newton division by Mr. S3cldon and his ardent baud of workers.

DEATH OF MR. H. B. WHITE.

It is with deep regret that we record the death of Mr. lienryßcown White, late magistrates' clerk for the borough, who passed away at his residence, is, Museum-street, at eight o'clock this (Friday) morning after an illness extending over three months. The deceased gentleman, who was seventy-one years of age, had been practically confined to the house for several years, the last occasion on which he was out of doors being in August last when he voted at the St. Austin's ward bye-election. Since Saturday he had been partially mum/miens and for the twelve hours preceding his death he was quite unconscious. He had been attended by Dr. Joseph. This cause of death was senile debility. The deceased gentleman was the son .of the late Mr. George White, of Manton, Wiltshire and he was larticled to his uncle, Mr. Stephen Brown Dixon, at Pewsey in Wiltshire. In 1856 he was admitted as a solicitor. He came to Warrington in 1851 as manag- ing clerk to the late Mr. James Nicholson, Bank-street, and in 1861 he entered into partnership with hirn.Later on, Mr Edward Nicholson joined the firm which was known until 1887 as "Nicholson, Brown and Nichol- son." The name was then changed to" B. White and S me." The late Mr. James Nicholson acted as magistrates' clerk for some years but owing to a dis- pute with the Bench he resigned and was succeeded lby the late Mr. Robert Davies. When Mr. Davies relinquished the duties in 1839 Mr. White was appointed and held the position until 1901. He discharged the work with conspicuous ability and during the whole of the time that he advised the Bench no decision of the magistrates was ever reversed oa appeal. On his resignation his son, Mr. George F. F. White, received the appoint- ment and still holds it. The late Mr. White was the first clerk to the old Warrington and District High- way Huard and occupied that °Mee until the Board :was merge4.l into the Rural District Council. For semis years be was vestry clerk to the Parish of War- rington and rendered valuable services in that capacity. He was a public notaw and was also pro- minently asssociated with the Freemasons, being one of the founders of the Lodge of Lights. He had passed through all the degrees of the lodge. In politics he was a staunch Conservative and took an active part in the 1803 election. He was a widely-read man and one of his chief hobbies was the collection of old books.

He leaves six sons ani three daughters. MAGISTERIAL REFERENCES. At the Borough Police Court, this (Friday) morning, the MAYOR, who presided, said it, was his painful duty Ito have to announce the death, which had occurred very suddenly, of their old I.magistrates' clerk, Mr. H. B. ' White. Mr. White had conducted the business of that Court for nearly 25 years and he had never met, a more straightforward and businesslike man. It was the most painful duty he had had to perform since he had beer. Mayor, to express, on behalf of his fellow magistrates, their sympathy with the bereaved family. Mr. Throws n said he desired to associate himself with the Mayor's expression. He was articled to Mr. White and for a number of years acted as his assistant in the Court and a more faithful master and a truer friend he had never had.
