ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH.

Annual Tea Meeting.

WARRINGTON ORCHESTRAL SOCIETY.

The committee of this young society is to be congratu- lated upon its enterprise in putting in evidence the capabilities of its members at a concert given in the Old Music Hall on Tuesday evening. Considerable interest was evinced by a roomful of discriminating and appreciative friends in the presentment of quite an ambitious programme of orchestral and vocal music. Although much of the materiel of the orchestra is known to us as forming part of other local orchestras, it wears an entirely new garb tinder Mr. Tom Boothroyd's conductorship. It magas refreshing as it is undoubtedly full of promise for the future, to note the increased intelligence, and therefore the great improvement,which marked much of the great orchestral playing heard on Tnesday evening, and one is driven to the conclusion ' that here cause and effect ere seen in the infusing by a capable conductor of something of his own high-strung personality into the methods of the bandsmen under his guidance. The opening March and the "Raymond ". overtnre were, on the whole, most satisfactorily per- formed. it would be well,however,to hold in due restraint the quality of verve which was the most conspicuous feature in the interpretations of the various orchestril pieces, because there is always a tendency for an exuberance of spirits to degenerate into mere noisiness. Greater delicacy and dahitines,, especially in the strings, would for. instance, have given enlianced charm to the accompamments to the piccolo solo as well as to the ensemhte in the Entr' ante "Sweet briar," both of which —in regard to the purity of intonation of the strings, and generally in the ready response of all to the con- ductor's baton,—were otherwise admirably rendered. The " Athalie " overture and Schnbert's " Unfinished symphony " were the most ambitious items in the pro- gramme, and the manner in which the various sections of instrumentalists grappled with the very real diPl- culties of their parts is deserving of all praise. The first movement of the symphony clearly suffered from what may be regarded as a temporary and accidental misfortune—the lack of .a greater body of 'cello tone than is possible to be got out of a single instrument— for here, in the opening unison passages for string basses as well as in the melodic section for 'cellos only occurring further on, a great responsibility rests. There were however more positive shortcomings in the sym- phony which, if they did not detract seriously from the allround merit of its performance were nevertheless needlessly noticeable. They chiefly concern the wood- wind, and while apparent in both movements, it may be useful to instance only the obscurity of the recurring cadence with which the bassoon preludes the before- mentioned cello theme in the first movement. Nor are I these blemishes alluded to in any captious spirit, for it. was with real pleasure that we listened to much of the orchestral playing in this, the initial attempt of the new society to interpret, is class of music for which we venture to believe there is a steel public demand. T3so piccolo soloist is to be complimented upon producing la silvery tone free from screechiness and windiness, a well deserved encore being accorded his render- ing of "Echoes de Bois." Mr. Arthur Bennett, the leader of the orchestra, played a violin solo which called for executant abilities of a varied char- acter. It is no small praise therefore to say that alike in the legato, the double-stopping, and the lighter and I more rapidly moving passages of the "Romance" (I 1 ttba y) he displayed a facility and neatness largely commensurate with its demands. Miss Woodward and Mr. Hannan contributed sonie songs which afforded pleasant relief, the first-mentioned being recalled for a meritorious interpretation of "there's a land" (Al litsen). Mr.liannan VMS manifestlynot in good voice and was therefore somewhat throaty in his singing. Yet he interpreted with fair suceess two of Stephen Adams's 1 popular songs,winning for the second ("Maid of Malabar") an encore to which he responded with "I'll sing thee songs of Araby." Mr. T. Taylor, it should be noted, played the pianoforte accompaniments with uniform good taste. We look forward pleasurably to further performances to which the Warrington Orchestral Society will doubtless feel encourseed by the reception of this first venture—one in which they may be assured of having gained the good wishes of Warrington's musical public.

TREE PUNTING IN STOCKTON

HEATH.

9bjections at the District Council Meeting.

"THE WORK OF FADDISTS."

The scheme for beautifying Stockton Heath by the 1 planting of trees, when proposed, was heartily acclaimed throughout the parish, and a ready and enthusiastic response was made to the appeal for assistance. Daring 'the course of the past few months avenues of trees have been planted In the principal thoroughfares in the ' 1 village and nothing remained but for nature to crown 'the efforts of the HOZI thong. The suggestion that there is a possibility of their work being undone will come las a shock to the promoters of the scheme, but, judging from the trend of the discussion on the question at the • meeting of the Rural District Council on Tuesday the contingency may arise.

The subject was opened by the reading of a letter addressed to the Chairman of the Council, Mr. S. I woodward, by Mr. William Hough, " Reddish,' Whitefleldlroad, Stockton Heath. In the com- munication Mr. Hongh referred to the "alarming Anrnber of permanent obstructions which had been placed upon the footpaths in this parish which In foggy weather are absolutely dangerous. On a five foot footpath (in Whitelleld-road) trees with iron guards have been placed, making it impossible to bold up an umbrella in wet weather: I will ask the Council what will be the condition of the road in twenty or thirty years when the trees have grown ? In addition the cost of repairs and scavenging will be increased and this is not desirable. I wotild ask the Council under what seztion of the Highway Act are they able to delegate powers to place obstructions on footpaths to a few "faddists?" Mr. Hallows remarked that the guards round the trees were only nine inches in width. As far as a few faddists were concerned there could not be a few but must be a lot as over two hundred trees, bought and paid for, had been planted. The gentleman who wrote the letter was sore because a tree has been put in front of his residence.

Mr. Whitley said that was not the only complaint, as he had heard about a hundred. There were very numerous complaints in the district respecting the 1 planting of trees on very narrow footpaths with the result that the walking space was curtailed and reduced to three or fonr feat. For years they had been endeavour- inn to make footpaths as wide as possible ' and to avoid all possible ob-tractions. No one enjoyed seeing an avenue of treas more than he did. It. wasoall right in a case like that of Grappouhall-road where the footnaths were from 15 to 18 feet wide. In other parts of the village the planting of the trees had occasioned many complaints on account of the width of the footpaths being so much reduced. When the scheme was first introduced it was understood that trees should be planted not on the footpaths 1 but in gardens and oa the banks of the Ship Canal, where there is ample room. As the trees bad been planted it was impossible on certain foot- paths for two persons to walk abreast. Umbrellas and ladies' hats were being torn by the spikes on the tree guards and thus hundreds of complaints had been made respecting the trees. Ha moved "that no trees be allowed to be placed on footpaths in Stockton Heath under eight feet in width."

Mr. Stokes : Are we to understand from your motion that the trees are to be taken down ? Mr. Whitley: Arrangements can be made to put the trees outside the roadway altogether. Mr. Hallows.: I think the matter should be referred Ito the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee as they are 1 the most concerned in the matter. Complaints have been made about Mr. Whitley's shop where many-a-one has tumbled over obstructions placed on the footpath. Mr. Whitley: I pay ground rent for the footpath and the Council can have it if they will pay for it. Mr. Linaker : I understand that the resolutionjuoved by Mr. Whitley does not go so far as to say that the trees shall be cut down.

Mr. Whitley: The nir ischiet is that it was not stated what width the footpaths must be on which trees are

Mr. Hallows : I move that the matter be referred to the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee. Mr. IVhitley : If the Parochial Committee will rightly consider it I will withdraw. Mr. Pilling : The members of the committee being interested in the scheme Mr. Whitley is afraid that he willnot get fair play. There is no doubt that some of the trees are very objectionable and that they are a source of danger. ' Instructions were given that the Council would allow the trees to be placed on condition that the sites were agreeable to the Surveyor but the Surveyor has hardly ever been spoken to and the trees have been planted without his consent or advice being asked.

Mr. Baxter pointed out that on Ellesmere-road, which he said, was 20 feet wide, trees had been planted on each side. Iu 20 years it would be impossible to take a load of bay along the road. I Mr. Woodward : If the trees have been placed in such positions that they are dangerous obstructions,with , out the consent of the surveyor being obtained I shall be one to go in for insisting on their compulsory removal immediately. The question was referred to the Stockton Heath Parochial Com.nittee on the understanding that they should deal with it upon fair and equitable lines. ;

ST, PAUL'S TEA PARTY.

The Vicar and the Education Committee The annual congregational gathering in connection with St. Paul's Church w‘ta held in the Parr Thtll on Monday evening. About 250 parishioners sat down to an excellent tea, their wants being superintended,bythe lady members of the congregation. The Vicar, the Rev. W. Brncecamp, presided over the subsequent proceedings, being supported by Mr. 1 IT. Smethurst (vicar's warded), Mr. John Righv (people's warden), and Messrs. IT. Collin and F. G. Bennett (sirl esmen). During the course of a brief address the VICAR said the nnnual sale of work in connection with the Church would not be held in the schools this year. They had been able to have a room- in the schools every year fOr the sale, hut this year the education authority abso- lutely refused the use of the buildinv, and the result was that they had to go elsewhere. Such was the state of things at present, but he hoped soon that something would be done by which they would be able to claim their rights. (Hear, hear, and applause.) They felt it to be most unjust to be treated in that way with regard to their Church schools—to be turned out, and not have the use of premises which had been built for them, and not be able to do what the builders and subscribers rave the schools for. (Hear, hear.) Tn conclusion, the Vicar expressed the hone that the parish would continue to proper. (Applanse). _

SMETTITJRST observed that the year, so far as St. Paul's Churah were concerned, bad been one of increasing success. The offertories had amounted to £425 55., or on increase of nearly £6 on the previous year. (Applause.) They had bad the pleasure of send- ing to the Infirmary no less a sum than £3O 3s. 4d—a total that had yet to be reached by any church in the town. (Applause.) THE CASE OF SMALLPDX.

Mr. Hallows said he would like some information with respect to the recent case of smallpox from Stockton Heath. Humours had been freely circulated in Stockton Heath to the effect that certain charges, which were very excessive, had been made in respect to the treatment of that case. He would like to ask what the cost had been ? Mr. Ashton : I don't know what the amount is as no charge has vet been made. Mr. Hallows expressed his satisfaction with the answer.

THE LATE ALDERNIAN HUTCHINSON.

The MAYOR said it was his sad duty to announce that ' since the last meeting they had lost by death Alderman Hutchinson, one of the oldest members of the Council, and one who ranked with him (the Mayor) in length of service, and no one received the news of his death with deeper regret than he did. He had been an old friend of his, he had been a good member of the Council, and had given every satisfaction. He moved a resolution tol the effect that the Council received with regret the announcement of the death of the Alderman, and desired to record the great loss the borough had sus- tained. appreciating his long and valuable public services, and requesting the Town Clerk to convey the sympathy of the Council to the bereaved widow. They all deeply deplored the loss of old members of the Council. All the old faces seemed to be passing away, and they did not seem to gat any better ones in their places. Alderman TINNTON seconded, and said it had been his pleasure to know the Alderman for many years. He was a kind and genial man, a true friend, and thoroughly conscientious. He knew no member of the Council who took more patience and care in his work as a member of the committee than Alderman Hutchinson. His special and practical knowledge was of great advantage to several committees, and enabled them to arrive at decisions : it was certainly an advantage to the rate- payers. He was a man whom they all admired, and he felt the Council had lost a straightforward, genial man, whose word was his bond. (Hear, hear.) Alderman FR aNCOMB supported, and said he had been on the most intimate terms with the late Alderman from the time he entered the Council, and too much could not be said in praise of his character and his kindness to his follow Councillors. (Hear,hear). The motion was carried unanimously. ELECTION OF AN ALDERMAN. SENIORITY RULE DISREGARDED.

At a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee the Town Clerk submitted draft petition against the provisions of the Lancashire Electric Power Bill, 1906, which was approved. Councillor Citatx'asked whether the committee did not consider it advisable to run three tramcars on the Manchester-road route. " WOULDN'T BE WITHOUT IT."

A CHESTERFIELD WOMAN SAYS MOTHER SEIGEL'S SYRUP IS THE UNFAILING FAMILY MEDICINE.

"For years now Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup has kept me in health, and I wouldn't be without it on any account." These are the words of Its. Charlotte Dearslev, of 7, Albion-street, West Park, Chesterfield. They form the opening sentence of a letter, that she wrote on August 19th. 1905, to the proprietors of Mother Seigel's Syrup. Let us look for a marnent at the facts upon which this high opinion of a well-known nvNlicine is founded. " When I was thirty years old (now some thirty years ago), and for long after" says Mrs. Driarseley," I suffered terribly from indigestion. Food, of whatever kind and no matter how small in quantitv,used to afflict me with such violent pain that I dreaded to eat. I was hardly ever free from headaches, and became dizzy with their long continuance. Want of food, and inability to digest the little that I forced myself to eat, produced flatulence, another source of pain. In brief, I was a victim of indigestion, with all its attendant miseries. " In this way I continued to suffsr for several years, being unable in all that time to meet with any medicine, or any system of treatment, that afforried me the least relief. At the time I was induced to try Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, I had abandoned all hone of a cure. But it is just there that I was wrong. What so many other medicines had failed to relieve Seigels Spruo cured. It eased ray pain at once ; and thereafter improved rapidly, and was soon restored to perfect health. PLASMON OATS.

At this season of the year oats or oatmeal have always been considered a highly beneficial breakfast food, especially for growing children going to school. and those who have a laborious day's work in front of them. WHERE WILL THEY STOP?

Friday's racing at Daytona. in Florida, was of a sensational character, the speeds at!ained being far in excess of any previously recorded in the history of the automobile. The day's programme commenced with a ten-mile race, which was won easily in 7min. by an 80 hp. Darracq driven by Vaughan. Next followed a series of attempts on the kilomkre record, in which the Stanley steamer, driven by Marriott, the 200 h.p. Darracq,driven by Chevrolet,the Napier, Celdrino's Fiat, and a six-cylinder Ford took part. The Stanley car covered the distance in 18 2-ssecs., which is equivalent to 195 kil. 650, or 12157 miles an hour. The Darracq's best time was 19 2-ssec., the Napier being third with 21 3-ssec„ and the Fiat fourth with 22 1-5 see. In a mile race restricted to steam cars the Stanley did even better, as it established a record which it is difficult to believe can ever he beaten. The time recorded was 28 1-ssec., which works out at 20545 kilonnitres, or 127.66 miles an hour, or over 185 ft. per second. -" Country Life."

THE SIMPLICITY OF TEIE COURT OF SPAIN. "Any diplomat at Madrid who applies to the Minister of State for permission to present his countrymen or countrywomen to the sovereign invariably receives a favourable response within ten days after the application is made," writes a contributor to the Windsor. " The , chances are that the applicant will be informed in the morning that in the afternoon of the same day. at six o'clock., his friends and himself will be received in private audience.

, Nothing can exceed the simplicity of the necessary, or even possible, preparations in the way of dress. Gentlemen who do not wear uniforms go in their ordinary eyening'dress, while the ladies can do no more than go with their hats on, in such costumes as would be appropriate for an afternoon tea. "When the palace is reached and the grand. staircase is mounted, the party is met by the courtly introducer of ambassadors, who. has now been in office for .nea.rly fifty years. By that functionary, the ininister,lns wife, and their friends are conducted through several of the State apartments, so that the beauties of the palace may be partially disclosed before the royal cabinet is reached. There a pause must be made if it is necessary to settle the question of precedence with prior visitors. , If an ambassador is on the spot, a minister must wait. , The latter may, however, by virtue of his diplomatic privilege, sweep in ahead of a grandee of Spain. "The introducer, who leads the way into the cabinet, after addressing the Queen as Senora—an elastic term of politeness applicable to married or middle-aged women in every walk of life—announces the visiting diplomat by his official title. He then, after saluting the Queen, presents to her the friends in whose honour the audience has been given. When all are seated in a group, formed as in an ordinary drawing-room, the Queen, with her rare grace and dignity, leads the con- versation in any one of the five languages of which she has perfect control." From an interesting article on "The Etiquette of the Court of Spain," which appears in the February " Windsor Magazine," a very notable number, contain- ing large instalment of Anthony Hope's new serial, " Sophy of Krayonia." in which the heroine arrives within the kingdom of Kravonia, which in some respects recalls the same author's latuous " Zenda," hut with important points of difference. Justus Hiles Forman's " Ulysses McCleod " is successfully piloted through a further adventure, and among the short stories of the number are exceptionally good ones by H. B. Hariott Watson, Tom Gallon and Frances Rivers. On the art side it includes no fewer than seventeen of the pictures of Hr. George W. Joy. beautifully reproduced, and the third series of" Vanity Fair " cartoons printed in the original colours, some twenty-five politicians being the present victims. NAMES THAT TELL.

A. Conan Doyle, Ruflyard Kipling, W. W. Jacobs Surely no magazine reader will begrudge the humble sixpence which will bey, besides other interesting matter, three stories by these famous writers, who all contribute to the February number of the "Strand.' There is a long instalment of "Sir Nigel," Conan Doyle's acknowledged masterpiece, and the second story of the series, entitled "Puck of Pook's Hill," by Rndyard Kiplinc,r is a very charming one. "A Distant Relative," by W. W. Jacobs, strikes the humorous note for which the magazine is famous. Among the numer- ous illustrated articles there is one which is most timely as well as interesting, namely. "The King of Spain and His Palaces." The marriage of the King of Spain with one of our own Royal Princesses is an event which awakens a natural interest concerning the country and the canital in which she will pass her life. The palaces of Madrid, magnificent as they are, have for all Englishmen at the present moment, as the home of the future Queen, an interest added to their own. It ie interesting to note that this beauthfully illustrated article was written, and the photographs were taken, by special permission, in order that readers of the "Strand nagazine" might be enabled to form the best possible idea of their impressive splendour,

That talented writer Florence Warden contributes a capital story, entitled" A Pair of Rogues," which is followed by one of the most interesting articles we have met with for a long time ' • it is entitled" What is the Finest Dramatic Situation " and gives the opinions of the leading playwrights of the country on the sub- ject. To most playgoers the finest moment of a good play is that in which they experience the deepest thrill. This, as we learn from the critics, is the" moment "of the piece ; sometimes its very excuse. the clout on whish the playwright has strung his three, four or five acts of dialogue and stage directions. Dramatic situations are of many kinds, and affect differently people of different temperaments. How they affect our leading playwrights is faithfuily described in this article, and the whole capitally illustrated. " The Chronicles of the Strand Club " are as amusing as ever, and include drawings by such well-known men as Harry Furness, Tom Browne,

THE NATURAL AND TEE SUPERNATURAL. The February number of the " Grand Magazine " gives us in " The Natural and the Supernatural," the first chapter of what promises to be the most interesting and searching investigation ever made into the mysteries of the supernatural. Mr. Frank Podmore, who is con- ducting the investigation, is not merely one of the leading members of the Psyschical Research Society ; he is, perhaps, even better known as the keenest living critic of stories of the marvellous ; the exposures he has made of charlatans are innumerable. When such a man, therefore, calmly and deliberately affirms his belief in the existence, under certain conditions, of visions, and in the possibility of communication being established between the brain of a dying man, for instance, and that of a friend hundreds of miles distant perhaps, it is quite plain that time has passed when a sneer and a shrug of the shoulders will suffice es an answer to such assertions. Many of the greatest living men of science firmly believe that the mind has dormant powers more extraordinary than the wildest imaginings of any spinner of fiction, powers, moreover, that may, and frequently do manifest their existence at the most unexpected moment. Currents pass from brain to brain, and set up sympathetic action much as do the currents of wireless telegraphy. One is not in reality a whit more marvel- lous than the other. What is really required is to clear the ground, sift out fact from the mass of ignorant superstition that has overwhelmed and almost choked it, and soberly examine the Jesuit. This the investiga- tion now inaugurated by the ," Grand Magazine " promises to do, and as all realders of the magazine, which has already achieved a world-wide notoriety, are cordially invited to contribute any unusual personal experiences, there seems every reason to hope that, in the Editor's own words, "The borderland of the known will be explored as it never has been before." The extraordinarily low price—OA—at which this excellent publication is issued, and the varied and intensely interesting nature of its contents, combine to give it such a wide field that its readers comprise people of every class and opinion. For ourselves, we can only say that we shall watch the result of this investigation with the keenest attention.

My Dear Children' As I mentioned last week, I now have pleasure in announcing particulars of the competition for our boys. You will re- member that a week or two since I an- nounced a doll-dressing competition for girls. Of course, this is not open for boys, and I think boys would hardly know how to go to work. I hope that during February many of you will send me specimens of your handwriting for this competition. The following sentence must be written out three times: I ant a member of the Children's Corner League." After writing this three times, add your name, address, and age. The following rules must be strictly observed : I. All competitors must be under sixteen years of age. 2. This competition is open to boys only. 3. All competitors must be members of the C.C. League. 4. All specimens sent must be written on one side of the paper. 5. All competitions must be sent in to the office of this paper on or before February 28th.

I am afraid many of you do not pay sufficient attention to handwriting and neat- ness when sending me contributions, as I have received many badly-written and un- tidy letters. Some of these I can hardly read, and, of course I could neither print nor award prizes to course, members; so you must try and do better in future. I have awarded prizes this week to Mary Plinston and to Nellie Keene for the con- tributions prinMd below. I have also sent out three •albums to my nephews for send- ing me in lists of twenty-four new names for our C.C. League. With all good wishes from UNCLE NED.

FISHERMEN'S LUCK. "I don't believe yuu will ever have a bite, Jules," said Louis, impatiently. "I wish I had the rod! Remember, when the twelve o'clock bell rings, you have promised to let me have _it,"

"Yes, I know," said Jules, gravely, with his eyes fixed on the float, and his hands firmly grasping the fishing-rod. "But I shall have a bite directly. 1 feel it coming." " Do you really? " said Louis, impressed by the solemnity of Jules' voice ; and, leaving his puppy on the lower step, he sprang up by his brother's side, and, leaning on his elbows,--he, too, gazed out on the river. There thy sat—those two little French boys—at the foot of the statue of Victory, with the broad Seine flowing at its base, and the crown of laurel which Victory held in her left hand close by Jules' head. Surely he was to be the Successful victor of some wonderful catch before long? And he was! It was a gusty day. The wind blew in fitful starts, and one of these gusts blew a piece of paper out of the hand of a. lady who was walking along the side of the broad parapet. It rose high into the air, whirled round and round. and then floated gently (lovva, down, almost in front of Jules' float. The lady was in a- state of great excite- ment. "Catch it!. Catch it, little boy! " she ex- claimed. " I would not lose it for a hundred pounds! Oh! stop it from floating away, if you can! " "I'm going to! " shouted Jules. "Its my catch! ' he said to Louis. "I told you it would come! " Jules threw the line—it missed! The river was slowly carrying the .paper away. " Oh, oh ! it's gone ! " cried the lady. "No. it isn't! " called back Jules, aiming carefully, .aird once more he threw the line. This time the float caught the paper, and he drew it gently towards the boat. Jules called to his brother:

"Jump in carefully, Louis. You can reach the paper. I will bold it steady with my rod. Be gentle—mind now—for, if it should escape, we may not get hold of it. again." Louis crept softly into the boat, leant forward, and secured the precious paper, which he soon handed, with a correct French bow, to the overjoyed lady. "You clever little fellows! " she said. " Tell me your names, that I may know whom to thank. 'I can never be grateful enough."

" There i nothing to be grateful for." laughed Jules. "It was just fishermen's luck."

"Fishermen's skill, I call it," said the lady,' kindly. The next day there came a beautiful fish- ing-rod for Louis, and a strong little watch for Jules. _ _

" fishermen's luck! " again said Jules, laughing. MARY PLINSTON.

iy Dear Uncle Ned,—l expect you are .ginning to think that one of your members las forgotten you, and is no longer taking .ny interest in the Children's Corner. Please lo not think that. I always look forward to Tuesday when the paper is published. I have been in for a Cambridge exam., and the consequence is that my time has been taken up for working hard at lessons. Well, I must write now and tell you how I have spent my Christmas holidays, and try to win a prize for my letter. We broke up school about five days before Christmas Day, and I am pleased to say that I won two prizes. We then had our break-up party, which I enjoyed very much. Then came Christmas. I had fifteen presents. It would take too long, I am afraid, to tell you what I had. I have been to five parties, all of which I enjoyed very much indeed. At one party we had a sort of Christmas tree, and every one of the children had two presents off it as well as crackers. At this same party there was a conjurer, who did very amusing tricks. At another party we first had acting, and then a drawing competition, which, un- fortunately, I did not win. Towards the middle of the holidays I went to a panto- mime which came here. It was "Aladdin." The play was very well done, a‘nd during the last scene, which was a wedding feast, different people performed.. There was a boy, named Abex, who was only eighteen, and was immensely strong. He lifted a dumb-bell weighing 5601b5. He then balanced himself between two chairs, and two men stood on him. At the same play a man made himself into twelve different men, such as Nelson and Wellington, under one hat, made into several different shapes, and in one cloak. He made himself once a French priest, which was excellent. I acted in some waxworks the other day, and I was a nurse in Mellin's Food for Infants. I also acted in a play called "Dear Friends." This is all that I have done these holidays, and we begin school on Saturday next. I hope you will like my letter. I have tried very hard to win a prize, and I hope I have succeeded, for I should so love to have one. —With love, from your affectionate little niece,

NELLIE A. KEE,NE

"Oh! how dismal it looked from daytime, when the sun shines through the cracks in the roof," grandma continued, "for now it was so dark, and ghostly shadows ::eeined to flit about in the dim light of my lantern. How cold it was! How I wished I had never left my cosy room! But we often repent when .it is too late. I must have slept for about three hours in that damp place, when I woke up with a start, horror-struck. What was that noise, that shouting and pumping? I had put my light out, fearing it would be all used by the morning, when I should want it to find my way out. Now I wished I had not put it out, as I could not find the matches; so I had nothing to do but lay and shiver, and pull the clothes over my head to keep the noise away as much as., possible.

, 'I had heard gossip in the village about the ghosts of some "pirate who used to dwell in this 'cave, and on certain nights their ghost walked through the cave from beginning to end, and that they: howled and shook their chains, and woe to them that passed near the cave at that time. These thoughts came as I lay there. Suppose they should see me, and know ,that I had run away from my aunt? What would happen? I did not believe it in the daytime, but now it seemed Possible; so I decided to get out somehow, if I could find the way. Once more I felt for the matches, but could not find them. I thought I would walk to the station, and there wait for the train in the waiting-room. 'The noise got worse and worse, and there was a cracking noise now; and so I would lose no time, but get out. I bumped my head several times against the roof, but that I did not care. I got to the opening, which seemed miles away. The cave was situated on a rising ground, so that I could see all around me. The entrance was hidden by a clump of trees and bushes. What was that bright red light down there, and those dark objects? My aunt's house was about twenty minutes'. walk to the cave, and, as I got accustomed to the light, I was almost sure it must be my aunt's house, as it was separated from the others. _

I will go and see what it is. They will not see me if I keep in the dark. I put my things down at the mouth of the cave, and went quickly down the hill, and was soon near my aunt's house. A man was coming along. and I asked him what was the matter. Oh ' he said, 'Miss White's house got on fire. It began in the west side ,of the house, where her niece was sleeping. Miss White tried to save her niece, but it was no good, because it was partly burnt out before it was seen, and we are afraid both have perished in the flames' ; and so he passed on."

(To be Concluded.)

ZULULAND RUBBER.

Zululand rubber is the product of a sort of vine knowa to the natives as Ibungu, which has been identified by botanists as " Lando& this Kirkii." This tree is the most important source of rubber in East Africa. The rubber is ol good quality, and worth 4s. a pound, when fine Para rubber was fetching 4s. Bd.

===== 0002601_19060210_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
ROSS'S GULL.

I The breeding place of Ross's gull, otherwise known as the Roseate gull, has for long been one of the unsolved problems of ornithology. Mr. Buturlin has now succeeded in solving it, for he • has found adult birds, young in down; and eggs in the Kolyma delta, north-east Siberia. This interesting discovery was announced at a meeting of the British Ornithological Club.

===== 0002601_19060210_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ARAGOTITE.

Aragotite is the name of a rare mineral found in quicksilver mines. It was first described as a new mineral from the New Almaden mine, Cali- fornia, in 1872. For a long time, although the mineral was quoted in text books and in dealers' catalogues, the only specimen seems to have been the one presented to the Californian Aca- demy of Science by its discoverer, Mr. F. E. Durand. In a paper recently read before the Royal Microscopical Society, Mr. H. G. Hanks describes a yellow hydrocarbon mineral from the Aetna quicksilver mine, Napa country, Califon Ma, which he considers to be aragotite. It is a honey-yellow, • transparent substance, slightly heavier than water, and composed of carbon and hydrogen.

===== 0002601_19060210_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTROTONIC PHENOMENA.

I That the ' phenomena underlying the electro- tonic effects of the galvanic current upon the nerve and muscle tissues of animal matter are due, according to the New York Tribune, to ' physical causes, is the latest discovery of Dr. Jacques Loeb. In previous researches Dr. Loeb found that sodium and potassium ions in solu- tion stimulated muscle and nerve fibres, and that calcium and magnesium ions acted antagonis- tically to the sodium and potassium ions. In his : last set of experiments Dr. Loeb has found that this phenomenon is due to the migration and concentration in the region of the electrodes of ! chemical substances contained in the tissues. He ' has found that ollates, palmitates and stearates, all of which are found in the tissues concerned, ! precipitate calcium, and that it is in this way got out of the solution, preventing. its inhibitory effect upon the sodium and potassium ions which I are left free to act.

ANOTHER :JOON FOR THE EARTH. An ingenious paper on the rings of Saturn has been contributed to Knowledge by Dr. W. J. Knight. Clerk Maxwell's essay that won the Adams Prize in 1859 demonstrated the satelloid nature of the rings. Dr. Knight suggests that the Jovian and Martian satellites and the swarm of minor planets were originally units of Saturn's ring system which have freed them- selves from the distant planet's attraction and rushed sunwards, to be captured by Jupiter or Mars and thenceforward compelled to pursue new orbits as satellites or asteroids. The Great Red Spot on Jupiter, he thinks, marks where one of these , wanderers actually fell into the giant planet, and the remarkable orbit of Eros, one of the minor planets, which occasionally ap- proaches neare to the earth than any other body save the moon, holds out to us "the pos- sible acquisition ,of a second moon by the earth."

===== 0002601_19060210_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
PECULIARITIES 'OF THE CHAMELEON.

1 The power of the chameleon to change its colour according to its surroundings is well known. -A more remarkable and less known peculiarity of this curious lizard is pointed out in a scientific contemporary. The chameleon, it appears, has the power of being awake on one half of its body and asleep on the ether! The nervous centres on the one side of the animal, in fact, work independently of those of the other. Thus, if a chameleon is approached when asleep at night, and the light Of a candle be al- lowed to fall one side, that side wakes up. The: eye opens and moves, and the side .begins to . change colour. The other side remains torpid and motionless, with closed eye, and does not change colour. When the chameleon is agitated : its movements are said to resemble those of two animals, or two halves of an animal glued to- gether, each wanting to go its own way. This is what makes the chameleon unable to swim. j When dropped into water it becomes frightened ! and tumbles about as if in a state of intoxica- tion. It is suggested that this is due to the fact ! that the ewo eyes convey, different impressions ; to the brain, and thus two courses of action are indicated. Hence the confusion.

A DOUBLE ANIMAL. A double animal of another sort is the remark- able Diplozoon paEadoxium," found as a para- site on the gills of the minnow and other fish. When hatched they are freo swimming and single, but if they do not find their way to the gills of a minnow in about six hours after birth they perish. Sometimes they continue single, but usually two of. them join together in a sort* of Siamese-twin arrangement, and grow together as a sort of double animal. Unlike the chame— tho diplozoon need not bo troubled with ! divided desires, for it always remains attached to the gills of its host. It belongs to the class of worms popularity known as "flukes," and sciontifically as " Trernatoda."

- THE LIFE OF A STAR. Imagine, if you can, says Waldemar B. ' ICEempffert, in the Pall Mall Magazine, a vast mist, extending millions of miles into the bound- less space of the heavens, and glowing with a heat so fierce that it cannot be measured by any earthly standard. Imagine this mist spinning with terrific velocity, cooling as it spins, and shrinking towards its centre as it cools. Imagine that this shrinkage causes a still swifter spin. ning of the fiery mist (which must happen if our theories of dynamics are correct), that the enor-- mous centrifugal force generated overcomes the shrinking action and flings off a ring from the mass, and that continued shrinkage causes the accumulated centrifugal force to fling off still other rings, each of which greatly contracts about its densest part into a globe. And imagine. lastly, that these globes, still gleaming with heat, in their turn hurl rings into space, which condense into smaller globes and revolve about their parent bodies. If you have imagined all this, you have imagined the BIRTH OF OUR SOLAR SYSTEM. It is not a process of a year's duration, nor even of a century's, this birth of a star. Millions of years are required—just how many we can but ! vaguely guess. Because the process is so inap- preciably slow, it struck astronomers long ago , that perhaps there may still be is the sky some : evidence of its, various stages. Examined by the spectroscope, the nebula of Orion is found to consist of an enormous volume of incandescent gas, partly hydrogen. partly nitrogen, partly an unknown gas; and in this great volume of gas stars are plunged. When it is considered that the gases of which the four stars are constituted are exactly the same as those of the nebula itself, we can no longer doubt that in Orion may be found a most , wonderful example of STELLAR EVOLUTION.

As a star, contracts from the surrounding i nebulous matter its temperature rises, and with this augmented heat occurg-a change both in the star's spectrum and colour. Red-hot iron is not nearly so hot as white-hot iron. By observ- ing the various changes in tint which the metal undergoes, the foundrvrnan is able to tell with considerable accuracy its degree of heat. A somewhat similar method of gauging a star's temperature, and therefore its age, is relied upon by tho astronomer. Colour, then, and spec- troscopic analysis enable the astronomer to esti- mate the age of orbs that are only beginning to exist as stars; and others whose

===== 0002601_19060210_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
LIGHT IS FAST FADING.,

After having coagulated. as it were, from a nebulous mass, a star assumes a colour that may he best described as an intense bluish white. much like at of the electric arc. Stars of that hue are, tlss•refore, in their infancy. Then comes tlm white stage, followed by the yellow, orange, and red—each succeeding hue; indicating greater celestial antiquity than the last. 13p to the yellow period the star as it contracts grows hotter and ' hotter. Then a gradual cooling takes place.

At what stage in its evolution•docs a star reach its highest temperature? Astronomers are not altogether in accoro op the point. The singular law which paradoxically holds that, as it coolt and contracts. a star grows hotter up to a certain point, coupled with Sir William and Lady Hug- gins's explanation, would seem to point to our sun as

===== 0002601_19060210_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
Tire. HOTTEST TYPE OF STAR.

After a star has passed the stage which our sun has now reached, the metallic elements in its structure increase in number and importance, while the gases dwindle away. As the • star reaches the red stag, carbon becomes particu- larly prominent in its spectrum. Ages must still Aapse before the star ceases to be self-luminous, and is converted into a gigantie blackened cinder rustling tnrougn space. tit the development that follows, the planets above us and our own earth afford striking examples. The last and most pathetic period is represented by our moon —frozen. desolate, dead.

SOUTH LANCASHIRE TRAMWAYS.

===== 0002601_19060210_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Probable Abandonment of Part of

the Scheme.

At the meeting of the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council, at Earlestown, on Monday, the ques- tion of the South Lancashire Tramways Bill before Parliament came up on the minutes of the Electric Supply and Tramways Committee stating that, unfor- tunately,at the present moment it was found impossible to raise sufficient money to construct the whole of the tramways authorised by the company's Acts, and it would be necessary for them, if they were to include all the tramways in the pre- sent bill, to ask for a much greater extension of time for some of them than they felt would meet with the views of the various local authorities, and under those circumstances the company decided that it would be better not to apply for any further extension of time in the case of those tramways which they would probably be unable to complete within a reasonable period. The Clerk reported that it was evident the company intended to allow the powers to lapse, and to abandon the proposal to bring the tramways through Earlestown and Newton. This indicates that the system from Leigh borough boundary to St. Helens, through Earlestown, a distance of about nine miles, will be abandoned—the shortest route between Liverpool and Bolton or Manchester.

THE PROPOSED NEW STREET.

HEATED TOWN COUNCIL DISCUSSION.

to be formed by the owners and not by the town. Why these people should be favoured in that way at the Corporation's expense he failed to see. He objected also on another ground. No reasonable attempt had been made—assuming the Town Clerk had authority to negotiate—to negotiate for the property of Greenall's and Griffiths's. Why should they compel the owners to arbitrate before they had had a reasonable opportunity of negotiating with the committee? He (Councillor Lord) knew what he was talking about, and defied contradiction. The MAvon: Anyone second this motion? Councillor PEMBERTON said he seconded. He was hoping some other member of the Council would have seconded, because it was very unfortunate for the head officials of the Corporation to take up the position they did. It was one of the difficulties that any member who paid attention to the Corporation business and tried to do the best he could for the ratepayers had to meet with. He did not like to use hard words but considering many of them came there and gave a lot of time, they ought to have every reasonable facility that any official would have to give who was working for a company or private firm. They found that at Local Government Board inquiries members of the Council for the first time got information they desired, and were thus made to look very silly in the eyes of the ratepayers and of the Inspector. He did not think the members of the Council and the ratepayers fully realized the importance of Local Government Board Inquiries. They were, as it were, the last safe-guard the Govern- ment had provided for the ratepayers to save themselves from any undue expense or improper expenditure, and to get.information they could not get in any other way. He said deliberately that the misrepresentations—to put a light word on it—that some of their o:ficials gave in evidence before the Local Government Board Inspector would not be tolerated in any private business for five minutes. No private firm would be humbugged with it. They would at once dismiss an official who tried that on. They would not have dust thrown into their eyes. Councillor HULSE : Rot. Town 613rk's Criticism of Mc Council. -- " DRIFTING INTO INDECISION AND HELPLESSNESS."

There was a heated discussion at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening with reference to the new street which is being constructed at the rear of Bridge-street. Councillor Lord had given notice to ask the Mayor, as chairman of the General Purposes Committee, the following questions I. By what authority was application made to the Local Government Board to acquire properties belong- ing to Messrs. Greenall and Company, and Mr. Griffiths on the north side of Rylands-street ? 2. If such authority was obtained, what was the date of same and when wds it confirmed by the Council? 3. By what authority did the Town Clerk enter into an agreement with Messrs. R. Garnett and Sons to acquire the necessary property and land of Messrs. Griffiths, Greenall and Hewitt, and to take down the same, and within two years to make a street 40 feet wide over a part of the hod so required? 4. Has any further agreement or agreements been entered into with any person or firms to open the Sankey-street end of the new street 40 feet in width ? If so, what are the terms of the agreement or agree- ments, and with whom are they made? The MAYOR said some of thes6 questions should have been sent to the Street Improvement and Lighting Com- mittee, because that Committee had to deal with the matter. He thought Councillor Lord might have waited until the meeting of the General Purposes Committee, at which it had been decided that the Town Clerk should present a report, but, unfortunately, he wanted to antici- pate. A letter had been received from the Town Clerk with reference to the matter. Ile had not read it, and he did not know what was in it. BRIDGE-STREET lirI?ROVEIEIiT3. --- -- Messrs. Buda!). Bras'. New Premiss.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist9]

Twenty-nine points to five was the measure' of Warrington's superiority over the mem of Millom on Saturday. The match was the easiest Warrington ; have engaged in this season, and on their form of ! Saturday Millam are quite out of their class in the Northern Union League. A year or ! two ago they were a much stronger club and possessed players of some distinction, but of late they have deteriorated considerably and in the present competi- tion they meet very few clubs they have any chance of beating. Warrington's team of Saturday, though I only a shadow of the one which won the cup was 1 unmeasurably stronger than that which Millom placed 1 in the field and from the kick-off the only question I was the size of Warrington's winning score. Cricket I figures were freely predicted, but Warrington did not ! unduly exert, themselves and the Millona men. escaped ! comparatively lightly with less than. 30 points against I them.

! The match was devoid of any very striking features. Warrington supporters were interested in Lees, late I of Oldham and Chadderton, who appeared behind the ; pack in company with Lloyd, and gave a capital dis- play. His quick, level passing, and ability to beat a man smartly, were favourably commented upon, and ; he should do much to remedy the deficiencies which Warrington have so long deplored at half back. Lloyd and he changed places for a short time, and it was quickly apparent that only one of the two was fitted for the outside position, and that was Lees. Hid partner failed entirely to get the threequarters going, whereas. I the work of Lees in this Tespeet leads one to hope that ; ere long we shall see Warrington displaying again the combination for which they were, last season, lamella. The crowd was well-pleased with the new 1 man's form, and though he is not quite the youngster some people persist in believing, he should prove a valuable player for Warrington for a few seasons to , come.

One noticeable point in the NEilom match was the failure of either Kenyon or Harris to score one of the seven tries. Certainly, neither player displayed any dazzling brilliance on the attack, but the blame I think lies more with the inside men. They have not yet got into the way of making the passing runs which Fish, Isherwood, and Dickinson were famous for, and the wings were usually bored on to the touch-line. Jones appeared to me rather slow ixt getting away, but Taylor played a grand game throughout, though even he failed to find. opportunities for his wing,

The hero of the afternoon was Jack Preston who scored a goal and three tries. He might have had I half a dozen goals, but when the scoring got pretty frequent the scorers took the goal-shots themselves. Harmer and Thomas displaying some ability in this line. Preston's three tries was a g,ood afternoon's work for a forward and pltioved that he was always in the front. His 200 lbs of avoir- dupois took a bit of checking when on the more towards the line. Berry at back was not , overwhelmed with responsibility, and consequently seemed a hit careless at times. He did many good : things, however, and the conviction is growing that j with perseverance he will worthily fill the places of j men like Hallam and Boscow. Be has the additional advantages of youth and fine physiqUe, and there is j no doubt at all that he has a big opportunity of earn- ing fame in the future.

Saturday was rather a'sensational day under both football codes, and surprises were plentiful. The event of the afternoon under N.U. rules was the victory of Hull at Broughton. No one dreamed that with the championship almost within reach, and such a fine record behind them,, the Rangers would make any 'this- take about getting full points against Hull. But it just happened that the visitors were at the top of their forrmand the hornesters the oppliite, and Hull won by 5 Points to 2 points. Hunslet went down at Wigan and Halifax at Leeds,and Oldham against Hull Kingston Rovers. Leigh, though they only- halved the points at Runcorn, now head the League, and seeing their Ismaller and less arduous fixture list they may very possibly secure the championship. If they do it will be a testimony to the unreliability of Om new system in determining the champion club. Leigh are to be congratulated, however, on the form which has enabled i them to win 15 matches out of 20.

MR. J. A. SEDDON'S VICTORY

LEIBOBR PARTY'S REJOICINGS AT WARRINGTON.

The Member and his Opponents.

The striking Labour victory achieved by Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., in the Newton Division was celebrated by his active workers in the Warrington district on Mon- day evening. An enjoyable social gathering was held in the Cairo-street schoolroom and the member, who was accompanied by Mrs. Seddon, attended and received the warmest congratulations. An excellent musical programme had been arranged and dancing was also indulged in with zest. Daring an interval several speeches were delivered. Mr. J. Podmore, jun., presided and said Mr. Seddon was not a stranger amongst them. He had been to Warrington on many occasions and they knew what he had done for the Labour cause. That meeting was held for the purpose of congratulating him and wishing him " good-speed "before he went to take up his duties in the House of Commons. I Councillor Stott moved : "That this meeting corn- , prising residents of Warrington and district congratu- lates Mr. James Andrew Seddon, M.P., on his election to the British House of Commons as the Labour repre- sentative for the Newton Parliamentary Division and expresses the hope that he may have a long and success- full career in the service of the people." He referred to the triumphs which Labour candidates had achieved in Lancashire and acid the Press and even a section of the public had been astounded at the pro- gress of: the Labour party as evidenced by the election results. They who had been in the movement during the last ten or a dozen years; who had stood at Bridge Foot and various street corners, know what was coming. "'ley had expected what had recently transpired. (Applause.) The members of the Labour party considered that a great deal of the credit for the overwhelming defeat of the Tory party was due to them. Though the election results had killed Protection and expressed the feeling of the country on the Chinese labour question, what was more gratifying to the Labour men than anything else was that capitalism had received its first blow. (Applause). That was the aspect which cheered Labour men more than the death of Protection and Chinese Labour. When their Conservative opponents found that it did not pay to attack Mr. Seddou on general lines they began to attack his personality (" Shame "). The Labour party were used to being beaten and they took their defeats like men, but the Tory party could not take a defeat as men ought to do. They attacked Mr. Seddon and said he was an atheist, an immoral man, a bankrupt ---in fact everything that was bad. He could tell the people who had made those statements quietly and the friends of Mr. Seddon who might be a bit unsettled in their minds about the statements, that there was abso- lutely no truth in them. They as friends of Mr. Seddon wanted someone to come forward and publicly repeat the statements which had been circulated'quietly, and then the Labour party would take action. It seemed to him that the Conservatives were not satisfied to be beaten but they wanted to discredit Mr. Seddon as much as they could. Councillor Stott concluded by saying that they believed Mr. Seddon lost five or six hundred votes because men who were fully entitled to vote had been left off the register. Mr. PRUST (Penketh), seconded the resolution ,land said the victory had been one for progresd and political purity. Mr. Cownti. (Sankey), supported. Mr. E. ENGLA-ND (Paclgato) said the district which he represented did its duty remarkably well in the election. (Applause.) They had, he continued, heard a great deal about the fears of Labour men entering Parliament. He believed that never in the history of England hadi there been such a" brainy "set of members of Parliament as had just been returned. They could congratulate them- selves that the electors of Newton Division were to be represented by a man of brains. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN, before putting the resolution, said some of those present had worked for that day for twelve years and they were hoping great things from the Labour group in Parliament. They knew that the representatives they had returned wore men of real grit. (Applause.) They were going into a place of great responsibility and they would probably have to encounter insidious forms of temptation. They were going to be put upon their trial and it behoved the electors to watch and yet be kind. Let the Labour members know that they had behind them a body of men supporting them and con- tinuing the work which had achieved such great success —the work of propagating the principles of Labour and Socialism. (Applause). The resolution was carried with great enthusiasm. Mr. J. A. SEDDON, M.P., who had an extremely cordial . . . .

irecsption, said it would be mere affectation on his part I if be said that he was not proud of the victory. They had had al terrific fight with unscrupulous foes. Although they had had old prejudices to break down and calumny and abuse to encounter, his workers had stood loyally and helped heroically to win one of the greatesttabour victories ever achieved throughout the length and breadth of the British Isles. (Applause.) In the hour of victory he would like to be a generous foe. He had no desire to assume anything but a gentlemanly and generous appreciation of those who were stricken down on the political battle field, but he thought the time had come when they ought to tell the 'gentlemen's" party that abuse was no weapon even against the liaboar party. (Applause.) He was not mindful himself of the remarks that had been made. For some years he had lived in the full glare and he fully expected the abuse of those who had no other arguments. But there were some -non whom one looked upon as political foes and expected to be gentlemen. ,There was one gentleman known by the name of Garnett. He supposed it was the same person who wrote a letter to him dated from Southport asking him some silly, absurd questions upon trade unionism and tariff reform. He told that Individual that he had no time to answer every silly question from" Dick, Tom and Harry " and that if he wanted any question answering he must come to the proper place—that was to the pablic meetings. Mr. Garnett did net come to his meeting but he (Mr. Seddon) was told that he appeared on the platform of his opponent. He was perfectly entitled to do that,and from subsequent events he (Mr. Sedckm) was glad ho ' did go on the other side. He would have been sorry to have had him on theirs, There were others. Some had said he was an atheist. Colonel Pillrington knew he (Mr. Seddon) was a member of the same church, and that he was only denied a hearing at that church in the early months of last year, not because he was an atheist, but because he was a Labour candieate and opposed to the influence that dominated that par- ticular church. (Applause). He had been charged with being a wife heater. (Laughter). His wife was present that night and he supposed that if she wanted a separa- tion order she never had a better opportunity than now. (Laughter). He had told them something of the class of abuse he had had to put up with. So long as their opponents wore barren in arguments and could not attack their principles he supposed they would have the miserable crew which must have been in the mind of Lord Byron when he said The Devil himself with these would fear to dwell And in their skulls discern the deepest hell.

• They had not, continued Mr. Seddon, come there to talk about the remnant. They had come there to strike a song of hop— n song which he trusted would be an inspiration to the industrial army of this country. For long years the workers had permitted their employers and the capitalists to be comfortably saddled noon their backs. They had seen gradually, but nevertheless surely, the condition of the worker growing worse and worse and they now heard the most Ominous sound that could strike the ear of the middle-aged man : "Too old at forty." They looked in the Workhouses—modern Bastilles and prisons to a great many of the old industrial veterans—and found men and women who in the hey-day of their strength had kept themselves respectable. Their only crime bad been low wages and when the eye was becoming dim and the stop feeble they had been thrown as so much old scrap into those modern prisons. Was that the end of government ? After a further reference to -present conditions Mr. Seddon denied that the Labour party were hindering the van of progress. The political parties of the past had had opportunities enough, but what was the condi- tion of the worker? The people had the power in their own hands and were determined to" ring out the old and ring in the new." (Applause.)Fle sometimes smiled when he heard the charge of atheism brought against the Labour party because it was the only party in the country that had introduced into its policy a humani- tarian and a brotherly instinct. They intended to work not for the extension of empire but for the extension of happiness in the homes of the people. They wanted greater comfort for the wealth producers and they said that the idler,whetherhe lived in the West End or in the East End, was a drag upon the coach of progress. Everyone who worked was contributing to the snm total of the happiness of the human race and ought to participate in the wealth according to his wants and the standard of living with which he was surrounded. Mr. Seddon briefly touched upon the questions of the feeding of school children and old age pensions and in conclusion said they were determined that the House of Commons should become the " workshop of the nation" to pass legislation to remove as far as possible the poverty and misery of the people living in these islands. (Applause). l'(efreshments having been served a snoce. took place, the following oontributin, eramme Wilkinson, Mr. aucl Mrs: . . . _

our OF A LIVING GRAVE.

of drain pipes, Ire, tin plates, " protection

News is at hand that the Ores miners, Forsyth an his two sons, who were so gallantly rescued from their living tomb on Tuesday night, have fully recovered from their terrible four days experience. Relating their sufferings, not the least of which was the frightful suspense created by their perilous position, they stated that when nourishment finally reached them they, were • buoyed with new hope. From early Monday until the houi. of deliverance they were fed by their zealous rescuers with a steady supply of Bovril and brandy under medical supervision. This in itself was a marvellous feat,for separating them from their friends was a solid thirty-six feet of rock. Through this a four-inch hole had been rapidly bored, ' by moans of which Bovril and brandy was lowered to them as the doctors directed. Chief Inspector of Mines Mcf.aren says it's the first case in the history of mining that emtombed miners hAme been fed through "bore holes," and Bovril was the first nutriment passed down. The Scottish press is loud in its praises of the excel- lent methods adopted to revive and sustain the ini- prisoned Colliers. One of the sons was unable to par- take of the food for some time,owing to exhaustion, but be has also since recovered. Owing to the wisdom of the medical men, al: three Forsyths are now about as usual, not much the worse for their thrilling escape.

THE MONTH'S MAGAZINES. THE STORY OF AN ELECTION WRIT. There is a Parliamentary life behind the scenes of which the public rarely or never hears, and one of its least-known phases is the complex and responsible work involved in the issue and collection of election writs. This is illustrated with special photographs and des- cribed by Mr. Alfred Kinnear, the well-known Parlia- mentary writer, in the February number of the " Pall Mall Magazine," as follows, so far as extracts can do the article jostle° : It is said that even as the curtain rises upon the pro- duction of one pantarnitue, artists are at work upon its successor a year hence. So with the writs for a General Election. No sooner has the last return of a new , election been received than the clerical staff of the !Great Said, in the office of the Clerk of the Crown. pro- ceed to prepare the writs for its successor. Neturally, it seems a gigantic undertaking ; yet the Clerk of the Crown- had made his preparations for the General Election of 1906 before its predecessor of 1900 had quite settled to the fulfilment of its destinies. The result is that the Clerk of the Crown has been ready and awaiting another General Election for more than five years. Writs have passed though some vicissitudes. One was issued for Norwich as the old and new sheriffs were in the transition stage. The latter claimed the right to execute it, and was challenged by his predecessor, who maintained that he was in office on the day the writ was issued. There were no railways in those days and stage-coaches travelled not rapidly. So a deadlock ensued, but the Crown authorities decided in favour of the new sheriff. Another sheriff, also of Norwich, for delaying the writ, was mobbed by the populace at the sheriff hall. dragging the frightened man forth with cries of "Kill him! head him!" But one John Damme —a name suitable to the occasion rescued the trembling wight, "or else he had been slain. In the days of Pitt the Great Seal was carried off maliciously, so that the writs could not he issued until, in the emergency, anew Great Seal was "forged." The writs for England, Wales, and Scotland number 542, which accounts, of course, for double constituencies embraced in one warrant. The writs for Ireland, which number 101, are kept distinct, are issued in Ireland itself under the authority of the Lord Chancellor and the Privy Council of that coentry, and are dealt with independently of the Chancellery at Westminster. As soon as the writs are ready, which is within a few hours of the announcement of the decision to dissolve Parliament, they are respectively addressed in strong envelopes and directed to the proper official—sheriff, mayor, or returning officer. The whole are then despatched to the General Post Office by one of the clerks to the Clerk of the Crown. He has been specially appointed for this occasion with the title of Messenger to the Great Seal. The writs for London are delivered by hand. As soon as the elections are over, the various return- ing officers send back the writs to be kept by the Clerk of the Crown until the close of the Parliament, when they are all despatohed to the Record Office. It is curious into what little bulk the writs for a General Election in the United Kingdom are packed. They are placed in a small iron safe, which in its simple design and in its modest proportions might have been the first experiment in this kind of security. The potential parcel scarcely exceeds the demensions of a quartern loaf of bread; but looking elsewhere at the mountain of canvas bags containing the polling hooks and ballot papers, one is reminded of Prince Hal's plaint upon this" intolerable deal of sack." The bags roll in for weeks. There is a daily procession of electoral sacks to Old Palace Yard, where they are received from all kinds of private and railway waggons, and shot into the receiving chambers of the Clerk of the Crown. Each is docketed with the name of the constituency, some of the bags being models of neatness, other models of disorder. The whole weigh from twenty-three to twenty-five tons: and they are detained for a year and a day in the cellars beneath the rooms of the Clerk of the Crown, when they are finally " dealt with."

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. A Huge Death-Rate The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Di: trict Council was held at Bewsey Chatubrs on Wednes- day when there were present—Mr.Brooics (in the chair), Dr. Murray. Messrs. J. Mather, P. (.ongland, J. Bmk- house, W. Houghton, W. I.4'orshaw, J. C. Sutton (cle7k.), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. W. Rerfoot (irsp36,or), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), Ff. Ff. Barnage (assistant surveyor), and Dr. Sepbton (medical officer). THE HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. The MEDICAL OFFICER reported that during the month there had been 28 births and 76 deaths. The death-rate was equal to 82.3 rer 1,00 as against 32. the average for the past thrc3 months and 463 for the corresponding period of last year. Sixty of the deaths occurred at the County Asylum, execnsive of which the death rate for the district was equal to 119 per I,l'lo. Never befo:e had there bc3n so many deaths at the County Asylum during the month. Mr. SUTTON : You must remember that the great bulk of the people are rent there to die. Dr. SEPHTON : That is not so much the case now as it was formerly. In earlier years only chronic and incurable cases were sent there but now acute cnes were receieved. MANCHESTER ROAD REPAIRS. At the previous meeting of the Council Mr Brooks asked what was being done to strengthen Manchester- road so as to prevent the damage which .it sustained by heavy motor traffic. On Wednelday Mr. Newcomt reported that he and the County Surveyor had inspect3d the road with the result that the County authorities had made a spe3ial grant of £3O towards the cost of strengthening the road and had protnLed to largely increase the grant for the coming year. They quite appreciated the damage done to the roads by heavy motor tragic and felt it necessary that special provision must be made for it. •
